Asian family philanthropy is ‘a lot more hands-on’—and more corporate—than the West
2026-09-07

Asian family philanthropy is ‘a lot more hands-on’—and more corporate—than the West

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Asia’s richest families approach philanthropy in much the same way they approach business: Managing things directly and keeping a close eye on outputs, rather than just writing the checks. That’s one of the conclusions from a new report from the Bridgespan Group, a U.S.-based philanthropy advisory group, released at the Philanthropy for Better Cities Forum in Hong Kong on Sept. 7. Asia’s family fortunes are still younger than the rest of the world’s. About 94% of the Asian families Bridgespan studied are in their first or second generation of wealth, versus 85% of families in high-income economies elsewhere. Around as many are still in control of the businesses that made them rich, compared with 68% outside the region. That continued ownership shapes how Asian families give. Business-linked giving dominates in Asia, used by 95% of wealthy families in the region’s middle-income economies and 80% in its high-income ones. Elsewhere, just 28% of high-income families give through their businesses. Most Western families instead choose to set up their own foundation: Bill Gates and Warren Buffett, for example, chose to set up their own foundations rather than give through Microsoft or Berkshire Hathaway. “The level of control that families expect to have over their giving—because they’re still so used to having that level of control over the corporate—is definitely a lot more hands-on,” says Gwendolyn Lim, head of Southeast Asia at Bridgespan and an author of the report. She traces the habit to the conglomerate era, when Asia’s tycoons built sprawling groups by spotting “gaps in the market” and grew comfortable running operations that had nothing to do with each other. When they turned to philanthropy, they saw similar gaps, such as non-profits lacking the capacity to do good work or governments unwilling to step in to take action. The result was the “operating foundation” that both funded and managed charitable projects. Western philanthropists, on the other hand, are surrounded by mature civil-society groups, and so are more content to just deliver funding through grants. Bridgespan’s report finds that families in middle-income countries are more likely to administer their own programs than those in higher-income countries. More than three-quarters of Asian family philanthropies partner with the government, versus 58% outside Asia. That willingness to work with the state is unique, Lim says. Asian families are used to dealing with ministries through their businesses, and thus see few qualms in doing so again through their charities. Yet Western donors are more wary of the government. “If you talk to American or European philanthropists, working closely with the government is something that makes them nervous,” she says. “Their faces change a little bit. They’re like, ‘Maybe we influence the government from an advocacy perspective.’” Bridgespan’s report notes that Asian families might fund pilot programs to prove that something works, before handing over successful models to the government to run at a larger scale. Lim points out that philanthropic families are “usually standard bearers who are able to experiment a lot more.” Another difference is that Asian philanthropists are more willing than their Western counterparts to report how much work they’re doing, using metrics like schools built or teachers trained. More than 80% of Asian families report their outputs, compared with 45% of families in high-income economies elsewhere. Yet relatively few families—in Asia or elsewhere—report outcomes, or how things changed because of what an organization did. “They go: ‘I don’t want to pay you to measure outcomes, but I want you to report on outcomes.’ That’s pretty terrible,” Lim admits. This week, Bridgespan also updated its rankings of the world’s largest corporate and institutional givers, based on average annual giving between 2020 and 2024. The Hong Kong Jockey Club, the city’s only authorized betting operator, tops Asia’s corporate rankings at $774 million a year, ahead of Tencent at $404 million. Globally, the Jockey Club is the only Asian entity in the corporate top 10, at No. 8, behind Deutsche Telekom and well behind Johnson & Johnson, the world’s largest corporate giver at $3.8 billion a year. Much of the Jockey Club’s money is funneled through the Hong Kong Jockey Club Charities Trust. The Trust gave an average of $705 million a year between 2020 and 2024, putting it in the lead among Asian philanthropic organizations, but just shy of the global top 10. The Gates Foundation leads the way among institutional funders at $6.5 billion per year. The top 20 Asian philanthropies together gave $2.7 billion annually; the global top 20, $21.4 billion. Both reports were commissioned by Bridgespan’s Funders’ Council, whose members include the Institute of Philanthropy, the Gates Foundation, and the Rockefeller Foundation, and were released at the Philanthropy for Better Cities Forum, organized by the Jockey Club. According to AVPN, a network of Asia-based social investors, the region faces a development funding shortfall of $26 trillion through 2030. Filling that gap will be a tall order. “We call philanthropy the first line of risk,” Lim says. “Philanthropic giving fills the spots where corporates may be too nervous to invest. And family philanthropy is even more at the vanguard of giving.” Yet the gap Asian philanthropy is being asked to fill keeps growing. The Trump administration’s dismantling of the U.S. Agency for International Development canceled roughly 83% of the agency’s programs, gutting development budgets in a region where USAID once spent about $860 million a year; in Indonesia and the Philippines, program values fell by 95% or more. Aid financing to Southeast Asia could drop by more than $2 billion, according to estimates from the Lowy Institute. Nobody, Lim concedes, can plug the hole left by Washington. “Governments can’t fill the gap. Philanthropy can’t fill the gap. There’s not enough money,” she says. Still, in the “Asian decade,” Asia’s philanthropists will need to find some way to pick up the slack. “This is the decade where our own people have to help our own people,” she says. Nicholas Gordon is an Asia editor based in Hong Kong, where he helps to drive Fortune’s coverage of Asian business and economics news. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Is flexible work limiting learning opportunities for young employees?
2026-09-07

Is flexible work limiting learning opportunities for young employees?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
David J Finch and Christian Cook of Mount Royal University explore the pros and cons of flexible working and the unforeseen impact for less experienced professionals. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) Six years after the Covid-19 pandemic accelerated the shift to remote work, hybrid and remote arrangements have become an established part of the labour market. About one in five Canadians now works remotely some or all of the time. For many employees, this shift has brought greater autonomy and flexibility. Time previously spent commuting can instead be spent with family or friends. Research has also found that hybrid work can improve employee retention without sacrificing performance. These benefits, however, may not be experienced equally. Remote and hybrid work may be particularly difficult for employees who are at the beginning of their careers, when much of their professional development still depends on learning from experienced colleagues. Our new report, ‘The End of the Water Cooler: The Hidden Cost of Remote and Hybrid Work on Entry-Level Employees‘, examines this problem. Think about your first professional job. Much of what you learned probably didn’t come from an employee handbook. You likely learned by observing those around you. You heard how they approached a problem. Someone pointed out a mistake before it became a larger one. A mentor invited you to a meeting. You asked questions over coffee. You learned who fixed what, who to trust and how things really got done. These are forms of tacit learning: knowledge that is difficult to codify and is often acquired through observation, experience and social interaction. The workplace has traditionally provided an environment where this learning occurs alongside formal training. Over time, these interactions help employees develop judgement, confidence, professional networks and an understanding of how their organisations operate. A study of software engineers illustrates the importance of proximity. Employees who sat near their teammates received 18.3pc more feedback on their code and produced higher-quality code. The gains were concentrated among younger and less tenured employees, who were still building human capital. There is also evidence that remote work is affecting who gets hired. Employers are hiring fewer young workers for jobs that are easiest to do remotely. An emerging global study found that by 2025, early-career workers made up a substantially smaller share of new hires in highly remote-capable occupations. When researchers tested AI as an explanation, its effect largely disappeared. The remote-work effect remained. Canadian hiring data shows similar trends, as entry-level professional jobs remain more likely to be on-site, while flexibility grows with seniority. Younger workers also appear to place considerable value on opportunities for development and mentorship. Only 23pc of remote-capable Gen Z employees in the United States say they would prefer to work fully remotely, compared with 35pc of older generations. A 2025 Deloitte survey found half of Gen Z respondents said they wanted a manager who would mentor them, while only 36pc said they were receiving that mentorship. This creates a potential mismatch between where flexibility is available and where opportunities for learning may be most needed. But experience must be acquired somewhere. If employers primarily hire workers who already have the skills they need, rather than developing those skills internally, fewer young workers get the opportunity to build experience in the first place. The effects of reduced informal learning can be difficult for organisations to detect. Join us to create the technology of tomorrow Life-changing career opportunities for you Think for impact with Liberty IT. Delivering global software solutions Informing, entertaining and connecting the world Employers can readily track completed projects, meetings and other outputs, but they are less likely to measure whether a junior employee has developed professional judgement, built relationships across an organisation or learned how to navigate problems independently. Research suggests that remote work can change those relationships. A study of more than 61,000 Microsoft employees found that company-wide collaboration became more siloed when employees worked remotely, with employees communicating more within existing networks and less across organisational boundaries. This is particularly relevant to early-career workers, who have not yet built extensive professional networks. Professional development depends partly on access to people who can provide information, feedback and opportunities. A return to five days in the office is not necessarily the solution. Research suggests that well-designed hybrid work can preserve flexibility without sacrificing performance or advancement. The question is not whether employees should work remotely, but how organisations should structure remote and in-person work so employees can still develop their skills. Employers need to design hybrid work around skill development, not attendance. Some forms of learning that once occurred informally may now need to be supported more deliberately. That means identifying which activities benefit from in-person interaction and making sure early-career employees have access to them. This also puts responsibility on more experienced workers and managers. Mentoring, feedback and exposure to how work is actually done are essential to developing professional judgement and learning organisational norms. Post-secondary institutions face a similar challenge with work-integrated learning. The value of a placement depends not simply on whether students gain workplace experience, but on the quality of supervision and their access to experienced professionals. Governments that fund work-integrated learning programmes should therefore place greater emphasis on developmental outcomes new graduates transition to the workforce. Hybrid work has given many experienced workers more control over their time. But preserving this flexibility cannot come at the cost of compromising the development pathways of the next generation. Whatever our role or level of experience, we all have a part to play in making sure those opportunities continue. Dr David J Finch is a professor for innovation and marketing at Mount Royal University. He holds a PhD in management and a doctorate of design. He is an active educator, scholar and practitioner. He has numerous academic appointments, including as a professor and senior fellow at Mount Royal University’s Institute for Community Prosperity and a visiting fellow at the Henley Business School at the University of Reading UK. He has also taught at the University of Calgary and Ohio University. He specialises in labour market dynamics, focusing on the increasing demand for adaptive and open learning systems. Dr Christian Cook is a professor of human resources and an academic director at Mount Royal University. Her teaching focus is in the field of human resources (HR) and her expertise is greatest in the areas of HR strategy, employee relations, leadership development, adult learning, employee performance, and organisational learning and development. In 2017, she was the recipient of the MRFA Teaching Excellence Award. Prior to a career transition to teaching and research, she worked for more than 20 years across several large, international organisations and has held the Chartered Professional in Human Resources designation for more than 20 years. Expectations for lofty valuations are backed up by Anthropic’s rapidly growing revenue, which reportedly hit roughly $11bn in the second quarter of this year – more than doubling the $4.8bn it recorded in the first quarter. The company posted a net loss of $42bn for the entirety of 2025. Despite this, backers expect rapidly growing sales to reach an annualised revenue of between $100bn and $120bn this year. As part of the IPO process, Anthropic is looking to ​finalise a $15bn revolving credit facility, Reuters reported. Sources further told the publication that Morgan Stanley, ⁠Goldman Sachs, ​JPMorgan and Citi are among the banks working with the AI giant on the IPO. Last month, a US judge blocked the Pentagon’s blacklisting of Anthropic’s products for government use, handing the AI company a major win ahead of its plans to go public. The company sued the US government in March after its refusal to lower guardrails on its AI models led to an abrupt ‘supply chain risk’ designation. In the 27 August ruling, the judge said that the Pentagon’s actions threatened to harm Anthropic and quell public debate around its actions. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Only 20pc of professionals anticipate a traditional retirement, finds report
2026-09-07

Only 20pc of professionals anticipate a traditional retirement, finds report

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
‘The findings point to a tension at the heart of the 100-year life,’ according to Irish Life’s research. Irish Life has published the results of its ‘Life100+: Exploring Ireland’s Longevity Opportunity’ report, which takes a closer look at how increasing expentancies are transforming life patterns, financial planning and career paths. In partnership with independent researcher Big Window Consulting, Irish Life collected data from 2,000 adults in Ireland and also carried out an employer roundtable, geared around the topic of challenges and opportunities that longevity presents to their organisations and their people. What was noted in Irish Life’s report is that only one out of every five who participated in the research expects to fully retire in what is seen as the traditional way aged 65. Nearly three in four said they will have to work longer to support themselves in their later years and this figure rose close to nine out of 10 for those aged 65 and older. Younger participants aged 18 to 24 said that they expect to extend their retirement age by making only minimal adjustments, adding around three years, while the older-than-65 cohort said it would take at least four additional years. All other categories added around one year. Comparable research carried out with a UK-based workforce found that harsher adjustments to future plans might seen as a wiser long-term goal, with participants there more open to substantial changes. Young people in the UK aged 18-34 expect to increase their planned retirement age by five years on average. The report argued that in Ireland and the UK, those closest to retirement appear to be willing to make the smallest adjustments, reflecting the constraints of changing long-established plans. For many participants who contributed their insights to the research, financial preparation and longevity go hand in hand. Roughly eight in 10 adults are aware of the financial planning that is needed for a longer life – 77pc would plan for regular retirement plan reviews and 70pc said they would save or invest more. Two in three would seek to transition into part-time or flexible work later in life and a similar proportion would work for longer before retiring altogether (63pc). Almost 60pc of young people under 34 expect to have to retrain or pursue multiple careers, thereby extending and diversifying their working lives. This group also expects to save and invest more. Comparatively, 72pc of 35- to 44-year-olds-are also considering greater future saving and investing. Older groups were shown in the research to have a preference for a phased retirement plan that involves moving into part-time and flexible roles (78pc). There was also a clear gender gap as men (52pc) were found to be more likely to anticipate pursuing multiple careers than women (45pc), as well as starting a business (52pc versus 42pc), undertaking freelance work (55 versus 48pc), or participating in the gig economy (48pc versus 36pc). Join a culture that offers a world of possibilities Always at the cutting edge of new innovation Despite the interest uncovered in working towards the future, Irish Life’s research also identified areas which those looking to prepare for retirement should study further, as more than two in every three respondents said that they had little or no experience of investing. A similar number of respondents said that they preferred bank deposits to riskier investments, particularly those aged 65 and older (75pc), and 58pc said they were uncomfortable with stock market swings. This discomfort was higher among those aged 65 and older, and for women more than men (61pc versus 55pc). The report said, “The findings point to a tension at the heart of the 100-year life. People know longer lives require greater financial resources, but few have plans in place or are confident in their ability to generate them. “A savings gap exists between what people believe they will need and what they actually have. Most understand the need to save more, but relatively few feel confident about investing, despite the role that long-term investment growth can play in funding extended retirements. “The challenge is about building confidence and financial capability. Professional advice, financial education and accessible investment solutions may be key to turning awareness of longevity into meaningful financial plans.” Organisations will also have to commit to making the workplace a more suitable environment for an ageing workforce, the report found. It said, “As expectations around work and retirement evolve, there is widespread recognition that workplaces will need to change. Almost all adults in Ireland believe organisations will need to adapt to a future in which people work later into life (95pc).” When asked how workplaces should respond, cultural change was found to be the most important priority for participants. This includes a focus on a more age-friendly culture, increased flexibility in working arrangements, health and well-being supports, and support for phased retirement. Declan Bolger, the CEO of Irish Life, said, “The 100-year life is a really positive prospect. A well-lived later life means more years with the people we love, pursuing ambitions and new experiences. Preparing for longer lives goes beyond individual responsibility and extends to the State, policymakers, employers and providers. “The importance of acting today holds true. Navigating what lies ahead is a shared undertaking to support people and workplaces across Ireland to unlock the benefits of longer, well-lived lives.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Rhysida leaks 5.7TB of sensitive Berlin state data in major hack
2026-09-07

Rhysida leaks 5.7TB of sensitive Berlin state data in major hack

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Leaked files contains identity documents, payslips and employment references, among other personal data. Cybercriminals claiming to be from a group called ‘Rhysida’ leaked nearly 6TB of sensitive data from the Berlin state administration after hacking the government department last month. The attack comes just weeks before Berlin is set to conduct state elections. Rhysida claimed responsibility for the attack on its leak site on 28 August, posting an entry titled ‘Berlin, Germany’, which contained nearly 1.5m files – sized 5.79TB – including personal information on more than 12,000 individuals. The data was leaked last Friday (4 September) after the German state refused to pay a ransom of 30 bitcoins, equivalent to around €2m. The Berlin Senate Chancellery and security authorities said they are conducting a thorough review of the data following the leak. “IT forensic experts working on behalf of the State of Berlin are working around the clock to examine the published data. Appropriate measures will be taken based on the findings,” an official statement from the Senate Chancellery said. Affected individuals will be notified on a risk-based basis, it added. Citizens who, independently of this, become aware “that their data has been published or is being used for criminal offenses such as fraud or identity theft should file a criminal complaint”, the statement read. The leaked files reportedly contain scanned identity documents, payslips, employment references and contract documents, as well as sensitive data on critical infrastructure. Rhysida is already sharing lists of filenames on social media, according to news publications. Meanwhile, Hamburg-based investigative journalist and weapons expert Lars Winkelsdorf said that the leaked files contain plans concerning national defence ranging from the federal government’s secret communication channels in the event of an apocalypse to defence-related companies and emergency plans developed by government agencies. Last month, France disclosed a cyberattack on its tax authority in June and July that resulted in the theft of tax data belonging to around 678,000 French individuals and businesses. Isar Aerospace launches rocket carrying satellites, in European first
2026-09-07

Isar Aerospace launches rocket carrying satellites, in European first

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Isar Aerospace’s Spectrum rocket takes off from the Andøya Spaceport in Norway. Image: Isar Aerospace The launch is a key step in Europe’s goal to compete with US-based organisations for commercial space missions. A rocket developed and controlled by German start-up Isar Aerospace has made history by becoming the first commercial launch to reach orbit from continental Europe. As part of the ‘Onward and Upward’ mission, the uncrewed Spectrum rocket lifted off from Isar Aerospace’s launch complex at Andøya Space in Norway carrying a payload of satellites and reached orbit in around four minutes. The achievement marks a major step in Europe’s ambitions to rival US counterparts for commercial space missions. This was Isar’s second launch attempt, as there had previously been a range of factors causing delays, such as weather conditions, technical difficulties and disruption by a rogue boat. The first attempt saw the rocket launch for only 30 seconds before experiencing a crash. Commenting after the successful launch, Daniel Metzler, the CEO and co-founder of Isar Aerospace, said: “Today, Isar Aerospace opened space from continental Europe. Launch continues to be the largest bottleneck for the global space industry and from today on, there is a true alternative for commercial and institutional customers.” He added: “We achieved within a few years what had taken the European space industry decades before. Europe now has sovereign access to space.  We have entered into a new chapter for European spaceflight. “I am incredibly proud of our team, that has made this success story from Europe possible. We will now focus on rapidly scaling launch vehicle production, deliver on our order pipeline, and meet surging global demand.” According to a statement released by Isar Aerospace, the company’s model is designed to build scalable launch capabilities. There is also a keen focus on building the “machine behind the machine”, which includes a vertically integrated manufacturing model and a high degree of automation in production. And with the opening of a new 40,000 sq m production facility near Munich, Isar Aerospace will eventually have the capacity to manufacture up to 40 launch vehicles per year. “All launches are special, but what Isar Aerospace achieved today is historic, the first European company to reach orbit with their own launch vehicle” said Géraldine Naja, ESA’s director of space transportation. “A huge congratulation to the teams involved, who with this launch also complete the first milestone of ESA’s European Launcher Challenge, looking forward to seeing many more European launches, including from Andøya Spaceport in Norway in the near future.” In July, Isar Aerospace was included in new data from Crunchbase that explored how organisations fared financially in Q2 of 2026, with European start-ups reporting the strongest quarter in four years for venture funding. Isar made the list for its successful late-stage funding efforts. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Irish Government issues research sector security guidelines
2026-09-07

Irish Government issues research sector security guidelines

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
The recommendations ‘do not introduce new obligations, but support institutions in understanding and implementing relevant existing responsibilities in a proportionate manner’. Ireland’s research ecosystem will be subject to new national guidelines on research security to safeguard the sector against intellectual property theft and misuse, foreign interference, and emerging security threats. ‘Research Security Guidelines for Ireland’, created by the Irish Government and published today (7 September), is aiming for alignment with international best practice while protecting innovation and strengthening global research partnerships. The guidelines outline Ireland’s national approach to research security, and how the risks associated with international research and innovation collaboration are identified, assessed and managed in a proportionate and context-sensitive manner, according to the Department of Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science. The framework is intended as enabling guidance – rather than a regulatory or enforcement document – for Ireland’s research performing organisation (RPOs), funders of research and other stakeholders in the country’s research ecosystem. The guidelines “do not introduce new obligations, but support institutions in understanding and implementing relevant existing responsibilities in a proportionate manner”, according to the document, and are based on key principles of: risk management rather than risk avoidance; proportionate responses based on institutional autonomy; clarity of roles and responsibilities; and avoidance of bureaucratic complexity that stifles innovation. Minister for Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science James Lawless, TD said: “Ireland’s research system is among the most internationally connected in the world. That openness is one of our greatest strengths but it must be matched by an ability to identify and manage emerging security risks. “These guidelines represent a significant step forward in ensuring that Ireland remains both open and secure as a global leader in research and innovation. “This is about protecting valuable knowledge, supporting our researchers and making sure our research system remains open and trusted. “Many institutions are already doing good work in this area. What these guidelines do is provide a common framework so that research organisations across the country can build on that experience and take a consistent approach.” The guidelines are the product of collaboration between Government departments, research funders and RPOs, and originate from a May 2024 EU recommendation that member states develop national research frameworks with clear roles and responsibilities. RPOs retain autonomy and decision-making on how to implement the new guidelines, according to the Government, which said it will act as a coordinator and support mechanism during the implementation phase of the initiative that is now underway. The guidelines are available to the public on the Government’s website. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Elon Musk Is Insisting He Knows More About Chess Than Chess.com

Futurism | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Is being the world’s richest man not enough for Elon Musk? He must also have us all believe that he’s a genius, sui generis, with a mind so mighty that it’s wasted on supposedly intellectual pastimes like “chess.” He’s so convinced that the board game is beneath him, in fact, that he’s begun arguing about it with… Chess.com, a leading source of expertise and chess AI. The inane saga kicked off yesterday on X — where else — when Musk responded to a post about all the possible moves you can make in chess. To a mind as omniscient as Musk’s, the number — a mere 40 orders of magnitude more than all the atoms in the observable universe — wasn’t sufficiently large enough to be interesting. “The actual number of moves that are not utterly stupid in chess is tiny,” he claimed, “and chess will be fully solved one day.” Here, Chess.com entered the fray by responding with a succinct rejoinder: “skill issue.” “Chess is hard for humans, but not for computers. One day, it will be fully solved like checkers,” Musk wrote, essentially repeating himself. “Between now and then, or even after, have fun. People enjoy many games/sports where machines are vastly better.” Indeed, humans who aren’t intelligent as Musk, in their feeble-mindedness and fleeting time on this planet, may enjoy activities such as games and sports, blissfully unaware that a hypothetical machine may hypothetically make mince meat of those activities one day. And yet, as Musk admitted, he’s choosing to waste his time “arguing with some random intern.” (“I’m a full-time employee,” the Chess.com account clarified.) Musk also went on about how an artificial superintelligence — something we don’t even know is possible to build — will “figure out ways to solve and compress that are far beyond what we could possibly comprehend.” Then he cited some conversations he had with his personal AI chatbot, Grok, as if that put matters to bed. There’s certainly an interesting intellectual debate to be had about “solving” chess — something that actual mathematicians have explored and tried to tackle — but Musk’s arguments seem to be driven solely by contempt. The board game is a long-held bugbear of his. Back in 2022, in a now infamous tweet, Musk wrote about how he stopped playing chess as a child because he “found it to be too simple to be useful in real life,” before extolling how Polytopia, a mobile game, was the superior activity of choice for the cognoscenti. He had something similar to say amid his battle with Chess.com. Responding to another account, Musk wrote that he “felt diminishing returns from chess in terms of transfer learning to understand reality.” Next time your opponent destroys you at chess, you might try deploying that excuse, too. When the Denver board of Water Commissioners approved stage one drought restrictions back in March, they did so facing some of the driest conditions in decades. According to the US Drought Monitor, the watershed sourced for Denver’s water utility is experiencing a “severe to exceptional drought,” while the Denver metropolitan area has been in a “severe drought.” As a result, residents and businesses have been asked to scale back. Denverites now have to limit watering their lawns to two days a week, chosen by address. And even on those days, customers are prohibited from watering between the hours of 10am and 6pm, Gadget Review reports. Yet as some 1.5 million customers honor the restrictions, one major customer is evidently refusing to close the tap: a brand new AI data center. In a now-viral video circulating on social media, an unidentified resident of Denver’s Elyria-Swansea neighborhood filmed the massive lawn of a data center being sprayed with copious amounts of water. “You better believe that they are watering their entire green with so many sprinklers that it’s literally making a giant pond,” the woman says. Denver residents see local DATA CENTER water its massive lawns while the whole are faces drought restrictions pic.twitter.com/rrj7vMrhpt The identity of the data center in question is unconfirmed, but it’s likely the new 18 megawatt facility owned by the cloud computing company CoreSite. At around 170,000 square feet in size, the mammoth facility recently went live in Elyria-Swansea, though not without sparking a wide-ranging debate around Denver’s zoning regulations. It isn’t known which tech companies are renting out space in CoreSite’s Denver facility, but elsewhere the company has proudly partnered with tech giants like Amazon, Google, and Microsoft. As for the water use, it’s impossible to say whether the lawn sprinklers were in violation of the city’s drought restrictions without knowing the exact time and date that the reel was filmed. It’s also possible that the facility is using recycled water to irrigate its grass, though that approach has proved disastrous in other cities. Going forward, Gadget Review notes that all lawn sprinklers will be fully restricted as of October 1, with a ban set to last until Spring of next year. If Denver’s data centers are still spewing after the cut-off, it remains to be seen how — or even if — local leaders plan to hold the facility’s owners accountable. In an affront to creeps everywhere, Meta has started remotely bricking the smart glasses of wearers who try to secretly record other people. Now, it looks like more than a few weirdos have gotten the ax. On Tuesday, Meta said it’s bricked the photo and video-taking capabilities on thousands of its smart glasses users, as part of a software update that was reported by Semafor. Seven million of the glasses were sold in 2025, making that fraction of a percentage translate to up to, but probably less than, 7,000 individual units. Many establishments are outright banning smart glasses, including popular nightclubs. Some feel strongly enough about the glass that they’re confronting people who wear them in public. Himel, Meta’s VP of wearables, admitted that it’s fighting an uphill battle. “It’s been a little bit of a cat and mouse game,” Himel told Semafor, adding that “I don’t think we’ll ever be done… There’s always new ways to get around the safeguards that are in place. Yes: the company has launched a new AI-infused electric toothbrush that features a “100k pixel macro lens camera,” allowing users — brushers? — to “see what their dentist sees.” The appropriately named Dyson CameraJet claims to analyze 28 images per second and employ an “AI machine-learning algorithm” to identify and target “interdental gaps.” In case you’re wondering how it can take pictures in the darkness of your mouth, a “stroboscopic light” ensures “precise illumination for optimal camera visibility.” It also hooks up to WiFi and Bluetooth, theoretically opening it up to all kinds of cybersecurity shenanigans if somebody were to identify any vulnerabilities in Dyson’s software. “By combining a camera, machine learning, precision fluid dynamics, advanced brushing technologies, connectivity and WiFi, Dyson CameraJet introduces an entirely new way to keep your mouth healthy,” founder and chief engineer James Dyson boasted in a statement. Of course, all of that sensitive video surveillance footage of the inside of your mouth is going to cost you dearly. The undoubtedly overengineered gizmo — which looks more like an undersized hair curling iron than a familiar electric toothbrush — goes for a whopping $499 and at least comes with two brush heads, a charging cable, and a “refill dock.” Naturally, Dyson will happily sell you a “proprietary non-foaming toothpaste” as well, which lacks a common synthetic foaming agent called sodium lauryl sulfate (SLS) to make sure the toothbrush surveillance camera isn’t impeded by all those suds. We’ll leave it up to you whether you deem the product, which allegedly required the input of 661 engineers, is worth the price of an iPad Air or roughly ten Oral-B Pro 1000 electric toothbrushes — a popular budget pick for those who don’t need AI to brush their teeth. In addition, the Federal Communications Commission is weighing reclassifying some drone technologies like lidar as “military grade,” Ars noted, which would restrict who can access these drones even further. Drones have a lot of other practical uses beyond law enforcement, obviously, including monitoring wild fires and assisting rescue operations. Amid heightened backlash against Flock, though, they’ve come to be seen as another surveillance apparatus. But weaning the US off Chinese drone tech is supposed to be good for national security, according to the Trump administration. One small problem: a single Chinese company, DJI, makes more than 70 percent of the world’s commercial drones, according to the NYT. Good luck replacing all those. Plenty of other cops are bellyaching about the tariffs, too, extolling how irreplaceable that awesome Chinese made drones have become to their operation. What we expect from the upcoming Apple launch

What we expect from the upcoming Apple launch

TechCrunch | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt It’s finally happening — this week, we are (probably) about to see Apple’s first foldable iPhone, more than five years after Samsung launched its first foldable phone. Apple’s annual iPhone release events are always interesting in one way or another, but this year’s presentation is one of the most anticipated in a while. As Tim Cook hands off the CEO role to former Vice President of Hardware Engineering John Ternus, people have high expectations for Apple’s next devices, especially since the company is embracing an entirely new form factor. While Apple is usually tight-lipped about its product launches, there are always inevitable leaks, giving us insight into what we might see during the September 9 Apple event. While the foldable iPhone is most exciting, we’re also anticipating news about AirPods, HomePods, and the non-foldable iPhone 18 line. If Apple has made its consumers wait over half a decade for it to enter the foldable market, its first attempt had better be good — especially since reports indicate that the phone will cost more than $2,000. At least it’s not as pricey as the $3,500 Apple Vision Pro headset, and unlike headsets, people actually buy smartphones. The good news is that people who claim to be early testers of the device have reported back positive experiences. According to Bloomberg’s Apple insider Mark Gurman, the foldable iPhone has a premium feel, fits nicely into a pocket, and opens up into a wide, iPad-like display. The phone is expected to sport a 5.5″ cover display, which expands to 7.8″ when the screen is unfolded. When folded in, the device will look shorter and wider than a typical iPhone. To make hardware that’s still sleek enough to pocket when folded, Apple has had to make some concessions. The device reportedly skips the telephoto camera, which could be disappointing to the kind of consumers who are willing to open their pockets for a phone that’s expensive enough to feed a family for months. However, the phone is expected to have a dual-lens rear camera system with a main and an ultra-wide lens, similar to what’s on Apple’s more basic iPhones. Rumor has it that Apple will include MagSafe, the magnetic wireless charging and accessory system, but that it will forego Face ID to save space, relying instead on a Touch ID fingerprint button on the phone’s side. This shouldn’t be a huge concession — touching a button is about as easy as looking at a screen — but it might take some getting used to if you’ve become accustomed to Face ID. Like other foldable phones, the iPhone Ultra is expected to support side-by-side multitasking, letting you run two apps at once, one on each half of the unfolded screen. Even though it’ll resemble a small iPad when opened up, it will still run iOS rather than iPadOS, though shouldn’t be cause for concern, since Apple’s newest chips are powerful enough to handle it. In fact, the same chip reportedly powers Apple’s budget MacBook Neo laptop. A pain point for foldable phone users thus far has been the dreaded “crease.” With other companies’ foldable phones, you can see the evidence when you open the phone that this isn’t your standard display. It’s a hard problem to solve and one that maybe isn’t worth the technical effort, though Apple has supposedly created a display where the crease isn’t as visible. If foldables aren’t your thing — or if you don’t want to spend double the price on your next phone — don’t worry. Apple is also expected to release some new devices in the iPhone 18 line. We’ve heard leaks about the iPhone 18 Pro and Pro Max, but not much yet about the standard iPhone 18, which is predicted to arrive six months after the September event. That would be a first for Apple — releasing the premium models before the standard one. Now that Apple is finally moving forward with its Gemini-powered Siri AI update, the company is also expected to refresh the HomePod, a smart speaker and Apple’s answer to devices like the Amazon Echo line. References to unreleased products, including new HomePods, were recently spotted by users digging through a macOS preview build. The most surprising find, however, is that Apple appears to be working on AirPods with built-in cameras. In that same macOS preview, researcher Aaron Perris found a video showing someone wearing the AirPods and picking up a book. “With Visual Intelligence, your world becomes savable. See something you like? Just ask me to save it for later,” a voiceover in the video says. While some users may be skittish about using a device that can see what they see — comparisons to Meta’s maligned “pervert glasses” are inevitable — we have some degree of hope that a company like Apple, which has built its brand on privacy, will do better. Gurman has reported that “the cameras essentially act as eyes for the Siri digital assistant and aren’t designed to take photos or video.” Instead, the earbuds’ components allow it to “capture visual information in low resolution.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Authors push back as publishers and agents make claims on Anthropic settlement
2026-09-06

Authors push back as publishers and agents make claims on Anthropic settlement

TechCrunch | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Some authors hoping to receive their share of Anthropic’s $1.5 billion copyright settlement said they received surprising emails this week — emails informing them that someone else was making a claim on their payments. Anthropic settled a copyright class action suit last year, after a judge ruled that training AI models on copyrighted material is legal under fair use doctrine, but pirating that material was not. The deal received final approval in July, allowing the payments to move forward. Under the terms of the settlement, the authors of nearly 500,000 titles will be paid $3,000 for each pirated work. If the book is still in-print with a traditional publisher, the money will be split 50-50 between author and publisher. If the book was self-published, or if the publisher reverted the rights by allowing the book to go out-of-print, then the author should get the entire payment. But writers have been posting on social media that publishers seem to be claiming more than their fair share of some payments. For example, mystery and thriller author April Henry asked, “WTF is HarperCollins playing at? They claimed one of my books on the Anthropic Settlement that reverted back at least 17 years ago AND on the same day I got a credit alert saying they had been added as my employer! (which they never were).” At the popular blog Writers Beware, Victoria Strauss wrote that she’s been receiving author complaints that fall into two broad categories: one where publishers are seeking payment for works that they no longer have a legitimate claim on (because the rights have reverted), and another where publishers are seeking a full 100% payment when they’re only entitled to 50%. In both cases, Strauss said she’s “reluctant to attribute to malice what can be plausibly explained by poor recordkeeping” — and she noted that some publishers have already said this is a mistake that they’ve asked Anthropic to fix. Similarly, Authors Guild CEO Mary Rasenberger told The New York Times that she doesn’t see this as “a grab by the publishers” and that she doesn’t believe publishers are “specifically trying to screw any author over.” Instead, she argued that this is the predictable result of bad record-keeping and a confusing settlement process. Strauss also acknowledged that any complaints she’s seen are just “a peek through a small crack in a massive wall.” “But the unusually large number of reports I’ve received over the last two days, as well as the fact that authors are reporting the exact same errors over and over, suggest to me that these aren’t the kind of routine glitches you might expect from such a large operation, but something much more wide[s]pread and systemic,” she wrote. And publishers aren’t the only ones seeking a cut of the payments. Strauss said she’s gotten complaints that a number of literary agencies are also making claims, which she said is surprising since “agents are not rightsholders in the books that they sell.” Author Courtney Milan (the pen name of former law clerk and law professor Heidi Bond) was more blunt in a post on Bluesky, writing, “Apparently some agents are trying to claim percentages on the Anthropic settlement, and I do not REMOTELY think they should do this, what the fuck, stop that shit!” Milan and the Authors Guild also shared more details about how authors can dispute their payment allocations. (One tricky issue: When the rights to a specific book reverted. In order for an author to make a 100% claim on a book, the rights reversion needs to have happened before August 10, 2022, which is the “download date” in the settlement.) Depending on which corner of social media you frequent, the Tesla Cybercab event was either proof that the company is a robotaxi leader delivering the future or an overhyped affair with little substance. Whatever your view, Wall Street wasn’t impressed by the Cybercab event in Austin, which was unusually quiet and lacked the festival-like atmosphere of past Tesla events. CEO Elon Musk didn’t even attend — a notable and puzzling absence, especially considering he has spent years arguing that Tesla’s future — and its value — hinges on robotics and AI. As senior reporter Sean O’Kane wrote before the event, “The Cybercab is Tesla’s fork in the road. This is either the moment the company begins to transform urban transportation as we know it, or the moment that reveals that, despite its many efforts, Tesla really is just an automaker after all.” The event did little to boost confidence, but I don’t put much stock in events or demos. The real test is whether Tesla can launch a robotaxi service safely and at scale. For now, there is no scale, at least compared with Waymo. (As of today, Tesla has registered 45 Cybercabs in Texas, according to the state’s automated vehicle tracker.) Another wrinkle emerged early Friday morning. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration opened an investigation into Tesla just hours after the company put its first Cybercabs, which lack a steering wheel and pedals, on Austin streets. Federal vehicle safety regulations require manual controls like brake pedals, though the Department of Transportation recently proposed removing those requirements for vehicles that are designed to drive themselves. Tesla chose to self-certify, the standard route taken by automakers for traditional human-driven vehicles. The NHTSA responded by tapping the brakes, although Tesla may choose to operate even as the investigation plays out. To be clear, there is precedent for the NHTSA’s action, and you can read all about it here. Curious about the Cybercab? Tesla published several guides that reveal a few important details, including that this gilded robotaxi is not for kids. Insight Partners, Entrée Capital, and other private U.S. investors participated in the round, the company said. Magna International is now investing another $35 million in Yuma Energy, a Bengaluru-based firm that operates a battery-swapping network for electric two- and three-wheelers. Newlight, a Bay Area startup developing a hydrogen injection system to make cargo ships more efficient, raised a $9 million seed round that included investment from lomarlabs, the venture arm of Lomar Shipping; BIRD Energy, a joint venture between the U.S. Department of Energy and the Israel Ministry of Energy; deep tech funds Undeterred Capital and CiRi Ventures; and Fusion VC, an accelerator for Israeli startups in the United States. Importantly, it delivers more horsepower and torque than its non-hybrid equivalent while improving fuel economy to 31 mpg (in the combined cycle). Kia tweaked the design of the new Telluride, and it did give me Kia EV9 vibes, although it felt more like a cousin to that EV, not its twin. A few of my positive impressions: It’s roomier than its predecessor and it is quiet on the road. It’s not the fastest three-row SUV out there and some critics have worried that Kia’s move from a V6 to a 4-cylinder engine would sap its power. But I found it zippy enough, especially for a vehicle of its size. Finally, I included a pic of the interior because I tend to focus on the UX and user interface of infotainment centers. This layout is not my favorite on the market, but it gets a few things right. For instance, it places a row of controls below the touchscreen for easy access. There is one design choice that I will probably never get over, even though I am sure I would eventually get used to it — and that’s the location of the gear shifter, which is a stalk attached to the right side of the steering column. A lawsuit filed by The Seattle Times and Newsday argued that with the advent of AI, the journalism industry could become “broken beyond repair.” The lawsuit described generative AI as “a snake eating its own tail” that could “destroy the very organizations” that produce the content it’s trained on. “AI products like ChatGPT and CoPilot are touted as producers of content, but in fact they are rapacious consumers, devouring human-authored content and delivering back to the world copies and derivative imitations of that same original content they consumed to achieve their commercial objectives,” the lawsuit said. Trump also posted an AI-generated video of himself slapping “America” over a road sign that had previously read “Welcome to New Mexico” and claimed that “many people” had suggested the name change. The White House has not announced any effort to rename New Mexico. TIME has reached out for comment. After returning to the White House last year, Trump signed an Executive Order renaming the Gulf of Mexico as the “Gulf of America.” The name has since been used by federal agencies and departments to refer to the sea south of the U.S., and was updated for U.S. users on Google Maps and Apple Maps. The President has also put his stamp on Washington, D.C., renaming the Kennedy Center by adding his own surname to the title. After much back-and-forth and legal complications, the Center’s board voted in August to reinstate Trump’s name on the building’s facade, testing a judge’s order from May that mandated its removal. Trump added his name to the U.S. Institute of Peace late last year, pointing towards his claim of ending a number of global conflicts since his return to the White House. Outside of renaming landmarks and buildings after himself, Trump has also faced backlash over a string of what Democratic lawmakers have referred to as “vanity projects” across D.C.

How to Read the Room
2026-09-07

How to Read the Room

TIME | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Every room has a parallel conversation running underneath what appears on the surface. It’s a pulse, a vibe, a temperature. This undercurrent of unspoken behavior delivers data instantly, but only if you can decipher it. Neuroscientists, psychologists, anthropologists, and linguists have studied subtle behaviors for years in a quest to unravel the mystery of how humans create meaning together. I’ve spent the last decade training others how to spot invisible signals, detect who holds power, identify which relationship will influence a decision, spot who has already made up their mind, and decipher who is lying. These are the very skills you need to read the room—and they are all learnable. Here are four science-based approaches to improve your ability to do this: Your mood influences your judgment, and cognitive load distorts what you see. Happy people tend to predict positive outcomes and underestimate negative risks. Stressed minds can find hostility in neutral interactions, according to research by Australian psychologist Joseph Forgas. Landmark work by J.A. Easterbrook showed that emotion and strain reduce the range of cues we can take in and process. Overload your mind, and you will misread the room. Heightened cognitive load, the baggage you carry into a meeting or conversation, distorts what you see. When your mind is cluttered with thoughts of a fight with your spouse or an unfinished project, you stop observing what’s happening and start reading others through the prism of your own stress. In this mindset, a flat “thanks” can become a slight, and a colleague’s distraction can feel like contempt. When a room feels like it’s turning against you, the first question worth asking is whether the room changed or your bandwidth did. Every gathering has a vibe. While that may sound like astrology, science tells us it’s brain circuitry, and that we probably catch each other’s moods far more often than we think. Yale neuroscientists led by Joy Hirsch paired strangers face-to-face and used brain imaging to track what transferred when one person watched emotionally charged videos while the other person watched only the video-watcher. The second person began to mirror the video-watcher’s emotional state, even though they never saw the videos themself. A 2023 study in Communications Biology timed the process: strangers watching television together synchronized their emotional responses within 850 milliseconds. The synchronization ran strongest during positive moments, suggesting joy travels freely while we keep our darker feelings hidden. The emotional state you bring into a room can also shape how everyone else behaves before anyone has consciously registered that you’re anxious or angry. An anxious person can make people rush or interrupt others more frequently. A contemptuous person can license cruelty and make dismissive jokes or harsh judgment feel acceptable. A calm person can reduce the temperature long enough for rational thought to return. The message? Manage your own state of mind before you attempt to manage anyone else’s. A group’s intelligence is not the sum of its IQs. Research by Carnegie Mellon Professor Anita Williams Woolley into the collective intelligence of teams found that group performance had less to do with average or maximum individual IQ than with collaboration. She found that strong groups have good communication skills, open minds, and an equal flow of ideas among participants. A room where one person dominates may feel decisive, but it may be losing intelligence in real time. Watch who grabs attention and who recedes into the background. Who gets interrupted? Who is allowed to finish? Who tried to get into the conversation and then gave up? The smartest thing to do, for the group, is to allow everyone to speak and to be heard. Amateur room readers imbue simple gestures with definitive meaning: crossed arms signal defensiveness, eye contact suggests honesty. The pros observe changes from baseline and look for clusters of behavior that point in the same direction. Science tells us how badly shortcuts deceive us. For example, many people believe that liars avoid eye contact. Researchers at an international airport asked 338 passengers from 81 countries to tell the truth or lie about their travel plans on camera. The liars held eye contact longer—deliberately, a beat past comfortable. Meanwhile, honest people, nervous under questioning, glanced away and looked guilty. Fidgeting is also meaningless as a single gesture. Let’s say someone taps their foot in a meeting or plays with their pen nervously. Maybe that’s their norm. The behavior only becomes a signal when their pattern shifts. The savvy room reader asks what triggered the shift from baseline and watches for a cluster of behaviors. Did a particular question cause them to shift their posture, stop their normal foot-tapping, and freeze their blink-rate? That’s worth noting. The question is never, “What does this gesture mean?” The better question is, “What does this gesture mean here, from this person, under these conditions?” Room reading is a powerful skill that’s part social observation and part emotional intelligence. As you start to see people and interactions more clearly, you’ll alter your own behavior accordingly. And when you do, many more doors will open because you won’t be guessing what the moment requires. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Merz Under Pressure After Far-Right Party Emerges Victorious in State Election
2026-09-07

Merz Under Pressure After Far-Right Party Emerges Victorious in State Election

TIME | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Ulrich Siegmund, leader of the Alternative for Germany (AfD) party in the eastern state of Saxony-Anhalt, speaks on stage with AfD co-leader Alice Weidel at the party headquarters following the Saxony-Anhalt regional elections in Magdeburg, Germany, on Sept. 6, 2026. German Chancellor Friedrich Merz’s party on Monday ruled out working with Alternative for Germany, after the far-right party emerged victorious in ​state elections in Saxony-Anhalt but fell short of earning an absolute majority. The AfD won 43.8% of the vote in Saxony-Anhalt’s state election on Sunday, according to exit polls. Merz’s center-right Christian Democrats (CDU) secured 17.2% of the vote. “This is the hardest defeat that the CDU has had for decades. Of course, this is going to have consequences,” Merz told reporters during a press conference in Berlin. The Chancellor tried to ease concerns over the stability of democratic institutions in Germany, as the AfD’s victory puts them a step closer to governing at the regional level, something no far-right party in the country has achieved since World War II. “I can reassure you, our institutions are stable, and our basic law protects this order, and the federal government, led by me, will make sure that the basic law is going to be enforced,” Merz said. “This message also goes out to our friends in Europe, as we know that Germany has a special responsibility because of its history.” “This election has further weakened the unpopular Merz. He was unable to fulfill his promise to significantly weaken the AfD,” Benjamin Höhne, a professor of comparative European governance systems at Chemnitz University of Technology in Germany tells TIME. “The CDU in Saxony-Anhalt had already distanced itself from Merz during the campaign. This [result] is particularly dramatic given that, for many, Merz was seen as a beacon of hope as Merkel’s successor.” The AfD's national co-leader Tino Chrupalla has appealed to CDU lawmakers to make a "centre-right conservative majority" in Saxony-Anhalt a reality, telling a German radio station that “partisan games really ought to be a thing of the past.” Despite the AfD's uncertain path, the surge in support for the far-right party—which ran on a platform of restricting immigration, ending aid to Ukraine, and floated the possibility of taking Germany out of the E.U.—is remarkable in a country where the legacy of Nazism has long kept the far right at the political margins. The party’s Saxony-Anhalt branch is classified by the state’s domestic intelligence as “right-wing extremist,” a designation that its leaders reject. “We have made history in this country,” Ulrich Siegmund, AfD’s lead candidate in Saxony-Anhalt, said after polls closed. “We’re taking our country back, it starts in Saxony-Anhalt,” he added. “A jolt is going through all of Germany.” The result has already drawn global reactions. U.S. President Donald Trump posted a screenshot of the projected results without comment on Truth Social. Members of Trump’s Administration, including Vice President J.D. Vance, previously expressed public support for the party. Tech billionaire Elon Musk, who spoke at AfD’s campaign launch last year, congratulated AfD co-leader Alice Weidel on the projected win. In response, Siegmund thanked him “for your support” and said he would “welcome the opportunity for strong and constructive cooperation.” Russian presidential envoy Kirill Dmitriev posted a screenshot of Trump’s post. He said in the caption,“Historic win for the pragmatic AfD that will put migration under control. The ruling Bidenthink coalition eviscerated,” referring to former U.S. President Joe Biden, a Democrat. The AfD is friendly to the Kremlin and has advocated for Germany to end support for Ukraine. Invoking the collapse of the communist regime in East Germany and the fall of the Berlin Wall, Herbert Kickl, leader of the right-wing Freedom Party of Austria (FPO) said, “As of today, the strong wind of freedom from 1989 is blowing through Germany once again.” Other European leaders expressed alarm over the result. France’s Europe minister, Benjamin Haddad, said the win was a “grave moment in Europe.” Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk said more bluntly, “In Poland, only idiots or traitors can rejoice at the triumph of the AfD in Germany.” The AfD’s platform in Saxony-Anhalt ranges from tighter immigration controls to a sweeping overhaul of the state’s education system. Among its more controversial proposals is a policy of “remigration,” which is widely interpreted as mass deportation, although the party says it will prioritize deporting people with a criminal record. It has also called for segregating children of refugees and asylum-seekers in schools, and ending war refugee status for Ukrainians fleeing the war with Russia. The party would largely be unable to enact many of its immigration policies in any individual state even if it can form a government, since many of its proposals would require federal action. But it could tighten policing and pursue changes to schools and cultural policy at the state level. The party wants to steer schools and cultural institutions towards more traditional and nationalist values. It has also advocated revising history curricula to shift emphasis away from the Third Reich and for schools to ban rainbow flags. Instead, it wants schools to fly the German national flag daily, sing the national anthem at school celebrations, and teach more Russian language. The AfD has also attacked Bauhaus, calling the influential school and design movement an “aberration of modernism.” Critics, including Germany’s culture minister, have said the AfD’s rhetoric echoes that of the Nazis, who forced the Bauhaus school to close in 1933. Other proposals include establishing citizen patrols and imposing a moratorium on new wind farms. The AfD capitalized on disillusionment with the political establishment and drew on regional and national pride, including “Ostalgie” or nostalgia for aspects of life in the former communist East Germany. That message found fertile ground in eastern strongholds, like Saxony-Anhalt, where economic disparities with western Germany persist decades after reunification. During the campaign, Siegmund frequently rode a blue Simson, a moped manufactured in the former East Germany, a move that drew criticism from the descendants of the Jewish family that founded Simson. The Halle Institute for Economic Research in Germany has warned that the AfD’s anti-immigration policies could deepen Saxony-Anhalt’s labor shortage and cost the state billions in economic output. Merz has also argued that an AfD victory could turn away foreign investment from the state. Founded in 2013, support for the AfD has grown significantly in recent years. The AfD doubled its national vote share in 2025, and is the country’s most popular party nationally, polling at 28%, according to a recent INSA poll. At the same time, support for the federal government led by Merz, which is struggling to revive a sluggish economy, has fallen to historic lows. In the same INSA poll, Merz’s conservative party had 20% support. In Saxony-Anhalt, 87% of voters were dissatisfied with the federal government, according to an ARD poll. Siegmund has said he wants to become state premier and eventually bring the AfD to victory in the 2029 national elections. While the election delivered the AfD a commanding victory, the party still lacks enough votes to govern alone. With 39 seats in the 83-member legislature, the party remains three short of an outright majority, and faces no obvious path to obtaining it. Germany’s established parties have refused to work with the AfD, continuing their “firewall” against cooperating with the far-right. State premier Sven Schulze acknowledged defeat but his party, the CDU, said it would explore talks with other parties about forming a multiparty coalition. The AfD could find unlikely support in the populist Sahra Wagenknecht Alliance, or BSW, which scraped into parliament with 5.3% of the vote. BSW has rejected the firewall and expressed a willingness to speak with the AfD, although it said it would not elect either Siegmund or Schulze as premier. Siegmund has thus far ruled out leading a minority government or adopting an informal arrangement that would leave his government depending on unsteady support. “If necessary there’ll just be new elections, then we’d just get 50 or 55%,” he told ZDF on election night. He said, however, that he would be willing to work with individual lawmakers or parliamentary groups. Thousands joined an anti-AfD demonstration on Saturday in the regional capital of Magdeburg. Some carried banners that read, “life is better without Nazis.” One protester, Laura Saalfeld, told AFP, “Whatever the results, the atmosphere will change. Far-right sympathisers will show themselves more openly on the streets.”

What to Know About the Miami Plane Crash That Killed 5 People

What to Know About the Miami Plane Crash That Killed 5 People

TIME | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings An Amazon cargo plane overshot the runway at Miami International Airport in Florida, killing at least five people, on Sept. 6, 2026, . At least five people were killed and five others were injured after an Amazon cargo plane overshot the runway upon landing at Miami International Airport on Sunday afternoon. Miami-Dade County officials said in an evening press conference that 21 Air Flight 7598, a Boeing 767-300 freighter operating on behalf of Amazon, crashed into several vehicles shortly before 2 p.m., local time. The National Transportation Safety Board and the Federal Aviation Administration said they are investigating the incident. "The airplane, which was operated by 21 Air, was attempting to land when this incident occurred,” Amazon spokesperson Kelly Nantel said in a statement. “We’re working closely with local authorities and officials to learn more and will cooperate fully with any investigation.” Nantel said the company’s “deepest sympathies go out to the families, loved ones, and all those affected by this devastating loss.” The incident has impacted operations in “America’s busiest airport for international freight and second busiest for international passengers,” according to the Miami airport’s website. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy advised travelers should anticipate significant delays and potential flight cancellations. A full ground stop had earlier been issued, but was lifted about three hours later. According to the FAA, Flight 7598, a Boeing 767-300 cargo aircraft, departed from Luis Muñoz Marín International Airport in San Juan, Puerto Rico. Miami-Dade Sheriff Rosie Cordero-Stutz said during the news conference that the crash happened at around 1:58 p.m. Data from tracking site Flightradar24 shows that the plane had a speed of nearly 130 mph when it left the usable portion of the diagonal runway. It’s unclear what caused the plane to overshoot, and the NTSB and FAA are investigating. The airport’s runways and taxiways were closed, and the aircraft was left disabled at the northwest end of the airport. Some of the runways were later reopened. According to Miami-Dade Fire Rescue, more than 60 units and approximately 200 personnel responded to the scene, arriving at the airport “to find an airplane that had stopped beyond the airport operational boundary with heavy flames and smoke showing.” Photos and videos from the scene showed black smoke billowing from the plane. Miami-Dade Fire Rescue chief Raied Jadallah said in the news conference that several people were trapped in their vehicles. “What we found was a very complex situation here,” Jadallah said, and added that responders also conducted search and rescue of the pilot and co-pilot who were trapped in the aircraft. Jadallah said there were a total of 10 people involved in the crash: five were killed, three are in critical condition and were transported to the trauma center, and another two were transported to a local hospital. It was not clear on Sunday evening whether those killed and injured were on the plane or on the ground. North Carolina-based cargo airline 21 Air began operating Boeing 767-300 freighters on behalf of Amazon in 2024. The company was founded in 2014. Keith Winters, the airline’s CEO, said in a statement on their website that they were “devastated by the accident” and that the company’s “deepest condolences are with the families and loved ones of those who lost their lives.” Flightradar24 data shows the plane involved in the crash was a 32-year-old Boeing 767. The aircraft model is used for both passenger travel and freight delivery. The first Boeing 767 flew on Sept. 26, 1981, and started commercial operations in 1982 with United Airlines. Amazon Air’s cargo network oversees more than 100 aircraft and over 250 flights daily. It contracts all of its cargo flying to other operators. In its fleet are Boeing 737 and 767 planes, as well as the Airbus A330. Citing details from aviation analytics firm Cirium, the New York Times reported that 21 Air runs eight planes for Amazon Air, while Air Transport International operates about 40 planes, and low-cost carrier Sun Country Airlines operates 22. It’s not the first plane crash involving Amazon Air. In February 2019, Atlas Air Flight 3591, a cargo plane flying from Miami to Houston, crashed into Texas’s Trinity Bay. All three people onboard were killed, and a subsequent federal investigation attributed the crash to pilot error. Sunday’s incident is the latest high-profile crash involving a cargo aircraft in the U.S. Last November, more than a dozen people were killed after a UPS plane crashed into an industrial area in Louisville, Ky. shortly after departing Louisville Muhammad Ali International Airport. Floridian lawmakers expressed their sympathies with the victims of the crash as they thanked emergency services in the Miami area. Republican Sen. Rick Scott said he and his wife were “heartbroken by this tragic loss of life” and credited “all the emergency personnel for responding so quickly to the scene and putting out the fire.” Republican Rep. Carlos A. Giménez, a career firefighter and former Miami Fire Chief, shared that he was “extraordinarily proud” of the emergency services’ response and said he would be “praying for all victims and injured.” Republican Rep. Maria Elvira Salazar said “Miami is with every family affected by this tragedy,” adding that “as we pray for the victims, their families, and those injured, we are also grateful for the Miami-Dade firefighters, police officers, sheriff’s deputies, and first responders who rushed to the scene and continue their work.”

Robert Pattinson Is Disturbing and Alluring in Primetime, a Sordid Movie About a Sordid TV Show
