Robert Pattinson Is Disturbing and Alluring in Primetime, a Sordid Movie About a Sordid TV Show

TIME | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In the early 2000s, if your idea of a cheap thrill was to see presumed sicko pedophiles caught on camera as they advance upon their ostensibly underage prey, NBC’s To Catch a Predator was your show. The host, Chris Hansen, had once been considered a serious journalist, spearheading investigative reports on events like the Columbine shootings and the World Trade Center attacks. But on the sleazy, sensationalistic To Catch a Predator, he was more of a vigilante showman. The show worked like this: A crusading organization called Perverted Justice would initiate conversations in online chatrooms designed to attract potential sex offenders. Then they’d be lured to sting houses outfitted with hidden cameras, where Hansen and his crew would await their arrival, ready to enact a pruriently dramatic Gotcha! moment, generally followed by an arrest. Nobody likes a pedophile, and without leaving the Barcalounger, viewers could revel in the self-congratulatory certainty that they too were helping to keep the world safe for the kiddos. Documentary filmmaker Lance Oppenheim’s first fiction feature Primetime—premiering here at the Venice Film Festival—fills in some background pixels on the show’s rise and fall, focusing particularly on an ambitious, egotistical, and at least somewhat delusional Hansen, played by Robert Pattinson. It’s 2006, and Hansen and his beleaguered producer Andie (Merritt Wever, bringing an edgy nervousness to the role of a woman struggling to stay afloat in the ruthless world of TV news) get a chance to move their show into a primetime slot. The stakes are high, but the potential rewards are great. Hansen’s hubris balloons, and he infects others with his own inflated sense of the public service he claims to be providing. To complete its mission of reeling in the bad guys, the show employs youthful-looking decoys posing as underage kids. One of these is an elflike performer named Del (singer-songwriter Phoebe Bridgers) who can impersonate either a boy or a girl; she passes some tips along to a nervous newcomer who will come to be known as Decoy Dan (Skyler Gisondo, in a performance laced with touching eagerness). Though he hopes to become a real actor and thinks this is his way in, he wants to quit when he sees how demeaning the gig actually is. But Hansen, with his crocodile smile and purring, almost sultry intonation, persuades him to stick with it, assuring him that he’ll be helping to cleanse the world of evil. Hansen has gotten high on what he perceives as his own power, and his ambitions grow even greater as he and his team try to draw in a “whale,” ideally a major government figure, rather than the pathetically sad—though still dangerous and repellent—lowlifes who tend to creep into their trap. These thankless roles are heroically played by an assortment of actors including Tony Revolori; despite his character’s unsavory and illegal intentions, his tremulous shock at being caught underscores the show’s legally questionable entrapment methods. Skyler Gisondo in Primetime Courtesy of A24 Hansen’s unfolding mania is one of the chief features of Primetime, and Pattinson’s performance is one of the only reasons to see it. As he practices dialogue that he’ll use in the show, drawn from the chatroom fodder that has lured the show’s marks into its trap, he relishes the prurient material a little too much: “I will undress you, you will undress me.” His theatrically precise diction is both disturbing and weirdly alluring. Still, it’s not exactly clear what Primetime is aiming for: it seems to be making the point that while sexual predators are unequivocally bad, the people who make trash TV—particularly a sordid show that’s masquerading as a means of protecting kids and meting out justice—are also immoral, scheming, and just all-around unpleasant. That’s a shaky equivalence. Oppenheim, director of docs like Spermworld and Some Kind of Heaven, structures the film as if he were shaping a mosaic from disorienting shards; sometimes the scenes feel jaggedly disconnected. He and cinematographer David Bolen have given the picture a grubby, grainy look, including sections that mimic early-2000s-style video recording. If you feel dirty watching it, that’s by design—and if you enjoy feeling dirty, that’s your choice.

The Last Thing Teenagers Will Let You Teach Them
2026-09-07

The Last Thing Teenagers Will Let You Teach Them

The Atlantic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
A strange thing happened to me in a deserted parking lot on a recent Sunday morning: My 16-year-old daughter actually asked me a question. I was telling her everything I know about reversing a car without the help of a backup camera. “First turn your whole body, shoulders rotated, right hand on the back of the passenger seat, until you’re looking out the rear window at the world behind you.” Her hands were on the wheel. Her phone was face down in the cup holder. She turned her body the way I’d suggested and nudged the wheel to the left, and the car floated backward in a woozy arc that wasn’t quite what she’d intended. “Wait,” she said. “Do I turn with both hands? Did I do that wrong?” I want to stop here, because I don’t think I can convey just how unusual such an exchange is these days—her listening to me and asking for help. Sometime in the past year, she stopped wanting anything from me other than my credit card and maybe my nicest suede boots. She definitely stopped wanting my instruction, my opinion, my take on anything, having concluded that she already understands pretty much everything. The talking is what I have missed. For 14 or 15 years, my daughter narrated in a fire hose of chatter—to me, to her father, to whoever. So much talking: about why the sun sets, the kid she was mad at, the plot of a show she was three episodes into, how she wanted to decorate her room. Around the start of high school, the talking thinned out, siphoned almost exclusively to her friends. Nothing announced the change. It went the way a baby tooth goes, loose for a while and then simply absent. When she got a learner’s permit, some measure of talking reappeared. It wasn’t fully restored, exactly, but confined within one small, sealed space that moves. Inside the car, she knows she doesn’t know: In no version of parallel parking can she wing it without asking for tips from someone who has done it 10,000 times. Learning to drive is something she cannot fake her way through. This wobbly uncertainty, this return to a kind of innocence, is the rarest condition in a 16-year-old, and it lasts about as long as a learner’s permit does. So my daughter asks, and listens, and we manage to simply talk in some of the ways we haven’t in a long time: about what her friends did over the summer, which kid at the pool where she lifeguards asked her if she could babysit them. For an hour at a stretch I get a version of my daughter that I had filed away as gone for good. But this is also, in some ways, the worst hour of my week, because the terms of a driving lesson are the exact inverse of the many other kinds of lessons that came before it. Everything else I ever taught my daughter, I did with one hand on the thing: I steadied the tail end of the bike, I held the knife and moved her fingers back from the blade, I grasped her hand crossing Seventh Avenue for so many years that my palm still expects to find hers at intersections. In the car, though, she has the wheel, and I have the fear. My only instrument is my voice, which is not connected to anything that stops the car. Parents say they’re letting go or loosening the reins or handing over the keys, as if adolescence were a series of small, deliberate releases, each one manageable. Then you hand over the actual keys, and the metaphor becomes a physical arrangement staged on a public road at 40 miles an hour, at which point it breaks. First there is supervised driving, and soon there is nothing. In between, the passenger seat is one of the few places on Earth where a parent gets to occupy two conditions at once: maximum proximity plus zero control. I have thought about this a lot from that seat, just 18 inches from my daughter, slowly understanding that being nearby was never the same as keeping her safe. Ever since she was born, I had assumed that being close to her was a form of protecting her. I could catch her if she slipped. In the car, that assumption comes apart, and all I can do is sit there with my fear. What surprised me is that the fear runs both ways. For the first time in years, my daughter is frightened of something in front of both of us rather than scared of something I might do to embarrass her—that is, afraid of me. That distinction turns out to be enormous. Since she hit adolescence, her anxiety, in my presence, has generally been about whether I would say something wacky (yes!), make a dumb joke (also yes!), or exist too loudly in a space containing her peers (duh). I have been the weather she is bracing against. But in the car, we are braced against the same thing, and it is outside the windshield. A massive truck backs out without looking; a cyclist suddenly appears. We both suck in air at the same instant, and her hand and my hand tighten—hers on the wheel, mine on nothing. Coming to a stop at a red light, she sees me sending a text from the passenger seat. “I can’t understand how anyone texts and drives,” she says, almost bewildered, the way a fifth grader might talk about someone doing calculus in their head. At that moment, she is using every part of her brain to keep a two-ton object between two painted lines, and the idea that a person could do this and tap out a message strikes her as physically impossible. I agree with her, but what I don’t say is that practicing will help her rethink this. Competence makes the risk available. One day she will be good enough at driving to consider being more careless about it. As she improves and gains confidence, the driving, for her, slowly grows less frightening and more thrilling. She presses the accelerator, her foot more sure, and we both know that she is on the edge of a huge new expanse of freedom and change—that driving lessons are not about a test to pass but a way to get farther than she has ever gotten on her own. The shared fear is temporary, and I can already feel the expiration date on it. Some Sunday soon, we’ll have our last lesson. And what I’ll miss isn’t the driving; it’s the asking—the tacit admission that she doesn’t know everything—and how she lets her guard down while she’s admitting it. When the questions end, she’ll be very much on her way, and I’ll be the boots and the credit card again, which, I remind myself, is part of the assignment too.

DSA’s Problem Isn’t ‘Media Training’
2026-09-07

DSA’s Problem Isn’t ‘Media Training’

The Atlantic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
American socialists are convinced that they have a media-training problem. In the past year, members of the Democratic Socialists of America, the country’s largest leftist organization, have won electoral victories in places such as New York City and Michigan, drawing a spotlight that the group has struggled to handle. Last month, the DSA’s co-chairs, Megan Romer and Ashik Siddique, gave several high-profile interviews that even many of their most fervent supporters agree were disastrous. The two leaders struggled to give straight answers to simple questions, and they defended outlandish policies, such as abolishing prisons and police. They seemed to present socialists exactly the way their opponents caricature them: as extremist, unserious, and unprepared. In damage-control mode, many of DSA’s defenders on social media are repeating the phrase media training as though it were a mantra. If only the group’s leaders had been better prepared to deal with media outlets, the thinking goes, they would have avoided this train wreck. The DSA’s communications director, Priscilla Yeverino, recently told Axios, “Our leaders aren’t politicians. They’re working-class people and new to doing media.” The idea that the DSA’s troubles on prime-time television are a technical matter solvable by the right training is surely comforting, but it is also wrong. The group has a bigger problem: It is led by unpractical extremists whose views are a far cry from those that its electorally successful figures, such as Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez and New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani, espouse. This disconnect is partly explained by a sharp division within the DSA. One wing engages in mass politics and includes AOC, Mamdani, and other socialists committed to democratic principles. The other wing is sectarian and extremist: It evinces little commitment to democracy and includes open admirers of Hamas, the Iranian regime, and historical dictators such as Mao Zedong. If the interviews with the DSA’s co-chairs were embarrassing, that’s a result not of a lackluster media performance but of the actual positions the group has embraced. A closer look at the interviews confirms this. Romer, who belongs to the sectarian wing, evidently did not know what the 2000 Camp David Summit was and could not say what “taxing the hell out of millionaires,” as she demanded, would look like in practice. When asked about authoritarianism in Cuba, she praised the country’s constitution and suggested that Americans should learn from it. Maybe media training could have taught Romer what happened at Camp David and what a tax policy might entail. It still wouldn’t have made the pro-authoritarian politics she shares with her faction of the DSA more palatable. In December, Romer’s Red Star caucus, a self-declared Marxist-Leninist faction of the DSA, posted quotes from a speech by Mao in “celebration” of the Chinese Communist leader’s 132nd birthday. Members of the faction study the works of Mao and the Soviet dictator Joseph Stalin as part of their political education. Red Star has declared its unequivocal sympathy for Hamas and praised the “good judgement” of “our socialist siblings” in Palestine for working with the Islamist organization and “external groups like Iran, Hezbollah, and Ansarallah under a principle they call unity of the fields.” In other words, Red Star is an admirer of Iran’s notorious “Axis of Resistance.” That Romer wouldn’t critique authoritarianism in Cuba is not a lapse but an expression of her faction’s politics. Red Star’s stated policy couldn’t be any clearer about this: “To publicly critique a government or movement that stands in opposition to the American government is to do the U.S. State Department’s work for them, from Iran to Palestine.” In other words, any state or group that opposes the American government should be beyond criticism. Liberation, the DSA’s “Marxist-Leninist-Maoist caucus,” likewise declares the United States to be “not a nation” but a “prison-house of nations, with the Euro-Americans being the jailer.” Unlike Romer, Siddique belongs to the mainstream wing of the DSA, but he is bound by the group’s statements and broader culture. The sectarian wing currently dominates the DSA: The group’s convention in Chicago last year rejected a resolution opposing “all governments that engage in the repression of democratic rights.” Thus, when asked repeatedly what he thought of Fidel Castro, whom the DSA had praised in an earlier statement, Siddique awkwardly referred to “inequality in the United States” and criticized U.S. policy—deflecting the question and offering sound bites instead of engagement, much as an establishment pol might do. David Brooks: Democrats became great by fighting the left This pro-left-authoritarian orientation explains why DSA electoral observers shamefully reported no fraud when they were dispatched to monitor the electoral sham organized by Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro in 2024. Those results were so bogus that even the left-wing governments of Brazil, Chile, and Uruguay couldn’t accept them. Yet at a DSA session on the subject, a pro-Maduro organization’s assessment that Venezuela’s electoral system was “one of the very best in the world” was presented as fact, as was the false claim that Venezuela’s main opposition candidates were “fascist.” The two wings of the DSA are committed to wildly different methods. Mamdani governs New York City partly as a technocrat, and AOC has sought to influence the Democratic Party from within. One of the DSA’s sectarian factions, by contrast, recently suggested that socialist lawmakers “should do everything in their power to disrupt any Congressional business that does not benefit the working class or advance the socialist program.” They should, it went on, “creatively abuse their powers to make it difficult to conduct legislative business,” for instance by packing the galleries of Congress with hecklers. This faction seems to view elected lawmakers as “tribunes” for the socialist movement rather than as people who, you know, make laws. The sectarian wing got the DSA to drop its endorsement of AOC and refuse to support Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential election. Red Star argued against endorsing AOC unless she withdrew her support for Joe Biden (then the Democratic nominee), and it refused to fundraise for the Democratic leadership or central congressional campaign. But ultimately, the DSA dropped AOC on the grounds that she was not sufficiently anti-Israel—a singular obsession of the sectarian wing. (The foreign-policy section of the DSA’s political platform discusses the details of only one country’s politics, and that’s Israel and Palestine, in which it calls for the self-determination of Palestinians without making a similar provision for Israeli Jews.) For all of the extremist wing’s influence, however, my years of research into the group suggest that the DSA’s members are mostly not members of any caucuses. The average DSA member probably has more in common with AOC than with Mao or Hamas, and the nonsectarians seem especially dominant in the group’s large urban chapters—such as in New York City, where the DSA has more than 15,000 of its approximately 120,000 members. More than 80 percent of DSA members in the Bronx and Queens voted in 2024 to endorse AOC. The sectarian wing came to control much of the organization’s leadership by manipulating internal processes, which is one reason it opposes organizational referenda and other one-member-one-vote procedures. The DSA’s mainstream wing includes thinkers who are committed to democracy, critical of authoritarianism (including that of socialist regimes, past and present), and who reject some of the signature policies of the sectarian wing, such as police abolition. These figures have been vocal in response to last month’s media fiasco. Eric Blanc, a labor historian at Rutgers, sharply criticized Romer for “falling into a trap being set by the establishment,” which seeks “to paint DSA as extreme.” Like others in his wing, Blanc advocates for adopting a Nordic-style social democracy as a step toward overcoming capitalism. This position is also shared by the Jacobin founder and former DSA Vice Chair Bhaskar Sunkara, whose 2019 Socialist Manifesto explores Swedish social democracy in envisioning a socialism for the 21st century. Reacting to the DSA’s recent media debacles, Ben Burgis, a philosopher and Jacobin writer, opined: “We don’t do ourselves any favors by refusing to say anything too negative about left-wing dictatorships targeted by US foreign policy,” especially “if we’re trying to argue that the failures of authoritarian state socialism in the twentieth century shouldn’t mean that capitalism is the best humanity will ever be able to do.” The group has failed to reassure its critics of its commitment to multiparty democracy, Burgis added. Thinkers in this wing of the DSA take seriously the imperative to reconcile socialist commitments to economic justice with democratic and liberal values. Burgis, Sunkara, and the economist Mike Beggs have co-authored a forthcoming book called The Blueprint: How Socialism Can Work in the Real World, which attempts to offer a “realistic model” of a post-capitalist economic system. Drawing on the work of the liberal philosopher John Rawls, as well as detailed economic research, the book takes the Soviet Union to task for a “record of systematic economic failure, not just political repression” and suggests a model that mixes social ownership with a significant role for the market. It also engages the arguments of well-known critics of socialism, such as Friedrich Hayek and Ludwig von Mises. This work puts the flippancy of Romer’s “taxing the hell out of millionaires” comment to shame. But if the likes of AOC, Sunkara, Burgis, and Blanc wish for the American public to give their notions of an economically rigorous and democratically committed socialism a chance, they need to break with the fans of Mao and Hamas. So long as they share an organization with the DSA’s sectarians, they can’t complain when the media associates the DSA—and American socialism—with the extreme positions that it formally adopts and that its leaders espouse. No amount of media training can solve that.

The Charges Were Dropped, but the Damage Was Already Done
2026-09-07

The Charges Were Dropped, but the Damage Was Already Done

The Atlantic | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
David Huerta sat on a steel bed too small for his burly frame in a Los Angeles jail last summer and looked out a small rectangular window at Dodger Stadium, where he’d once spent evenings with his two boys. He was the kid who’d made good, although it hadn’t been easy. His mother died from breast cancer when he was 4. He was raised in southeast L.A. by his dad, a veteran of the Korean War turned Teamster, who paid the bills by loading and unloading cargo—and, when he was out of work, took his sons with him to the hiring hall. Huerta steered clear of gangs and instead went to college, became a union organizer, and worked his way up to become president of the largest labor union in the state, the Service Employees International Union of California. He was sure that he hadn’t done anything illegal. He had screamed at masked federal agents as they executed search warrants at a downtown apparel business that was suspected of hiring undocumented immigrants. He had been angry, no question—especially at President Trump. He had flipped his middle fingers at the masked federal officers. At one point, he sat down in front of the business’s gate; later, he encouraged other protesters to form a picket circle in front of it. When an officer warned him he might be arrested, he replied, “I can’t hear you through your fucking mask.” And when a police van tried to enter, he stood with his hands on his hips nearby. He considered all of that well within the bounds of his First Amendment rights. But he couldn’t stop second-guessing his decision to show up at such a volatile scene alone. Huerta wondered how his wife and sons, his niece and nephew, and his other family members would react to seeing videos of his arrest. He wondered if he had somehow jeopardized the safety of his union’s hundreds of thousands of members by drawing unwanted headlines. He wondered if his arrest would make others more fearful of opposing this administration’s actions. He wondered when he would be released—or if he would be. He wondered if he could survive prison, should it come to that. And he wondered what his late dad would’ve thought of him sitting on a thin, stained mattress as his cellmate on the top bunk managed a comedown from drugs. Would he have been ashamed, or proud? Trump’s top federal prosecutor in Los Angeles charged Huerta with conspiracy to impede a federal officer—a felony, under a Civil War–era statute that had rarely been used before last year. If convicted, Huerta faced up to six years in prison. But months later, prosecutors lessened the charges to a misdemeanor count of obstructing, resisting, or opposing a federal officer. This July, the Justice Department said it would drop the charges altogether, adding it to the growing list of federal prosecutions that have fallen apart before going to trial. Although those dismissals have been celebrated as victories, the cases have taken a financial and psychological toll on the defendants. For Huerta, the past 15 months have been filled with anxiety. He has endured sleepless nights and grown emotionally distant from his wife of 30 years. The public attention continues to make it more difficult to do his job. And despite his pride in standing for what he believed in, he struggles with the shame of being jailed and appearing in court in shackles—an image he worries that his two sons and niece and nephew (whom he has raised as his own) will never forget. As we spoke inside a coffee shop in his neighborhood of Highland Park one recent afternoon, Huerta’s voice rose in anger and indignation while he recounted and, at times, reenacted the minutes before he was shoved to the ground by a federal officer. He teared up. “The president of the union just got fucking arrested,” he said. “If they can do this to me, imagine what they’re doing to other people.” On the morning of June 6, 2025, the phones at the SEIU office were ringing with reported sightings of immigration-enforcement operations in and around downtown L.A. Huerta and his team—along with other labor leaders, Democrats, and social-justice groups—had been preparing for a moment like this ever since Trump was elected to a second term. The administration was trying to detain and deport as many immigrants as possible. Workplaces were being targeted, and even American-born Latinos were getting caught up in immigration dragnets. Huerta and his team would later learn that this day was the opening of a monthslong operation in the L.A. area overseen by then–Border Patrol Commander Gregory Bovino. It aimed to not just increase arrest numbers but also intimidate immigrant communities. Read: A U.S. citizen detained by ICE for three days tells his story The union’s rapid-response network was designed to help employees during moments like this. “Shit’s happening,” Huerta recalled thinking that morning, “and we’re now in the fight.” He heard a report of an immigration raid in the apparel district, so he scrapped his plans and headed there. Federal agents were swarming an industrial complex. A woman stood outside the facility’s gate, prosecutors later claimed in charging documents, filming officers and telling someone by phone what was happening. A federal agent later said that Huerta seemed to be communicating with that woman and other protesters “in a concerted effort to disrupt” the operation. As the crowd grew, people screamed expletives at the officers, and someone yelled, “I want you to kill yourself,” according to the accounts of federal agents contained in court filings. The agents described Huerta joining in the taunting, urging protesters to “walk in circles and picket,” and pacing in front of the gate, “effectively preventing law enforcement vehicles from entering or exiting.” Huerta told me that he had never acted quite like this at a protest, but that he had snapped when he saw how the officers were treating a young girl who was crying about her dad, who she said was inside the building. “They were so dismissive of her,” he said. He screamed at the agents, asking how targeting workers was making the city safer, and telling them that they were “taking orders from a fucking monster.” When a federal agent ordered Huerta to move away from the gate, he remained in place, confident that the vehicles had plenty of room. An officer then began to move Huerta out of the way. He recalls not fighting back and keeping his hands on his hips—just as he was trained. But he was still pushed to the ground. A video shows Huerta falling on his back, his head hitting the curb. He told me that he was rolled onto his stomach, sprayed with a chemical irritant, cuffed, and taken to the hospital, where he spent several hours before being transferred to a federal detention center. His wife, Lucía Aguayo-Huerta, learned of his arrest while at the doctor. His oldest son, Ismael, heard from a cousin, who was crying hysterically. He jumped on Instagram and typed in his father’s name. Huerta had a bum shoulder from an old skiing injury. His other son, Camilo, watched as Huerta’s arm was pulled behind his back in one of the videos. “I was almost on the verge of tears,” he told me. “Almost screaming at my phone, like, You’re fucking with his shoulder!” Huerta didn’t sleep that first night in jail, and when he arrived at breakfast, he squirreled away packaged food in case it would become valuable in the days ahead. He’d been fingerprinted for detention on a Friday night but had not been fully processed and so had no way to call his family, although he’d briefly called his wife, Lucía, while he was at the hospital. Protests had erupted in parts of downtown Los Angeles as word of the raids spread. Outside the detention facility, people were showing up in large numbers, clashing at times with police. Inside, news of those protests streamed on a TV. At one point, an inmate looked back and forth from the TV to Huerta, and asked Huerta if that was him on-screen. “That’s me, bro,” Huerta replied. He said the man asked, You’re the fucking union president? What the fuck are you doing here? His title, and years spent helping workers, made his life over the next 48 hours easier. Another inmate gave him toilet paper, a cup, and toothpaste, the holy trinity of jail provisions. Word spread, Huerta told me, and soon 100 or so men had gathered to applaud him. It was a demonstration of support that Huerta hadn’t realized he needed. The sense of community he felt in that moment reminded him of his own union and the solidarity shown by his dad’s old Teamster buddies, who’d rallied around their family after his mom died, bringing them money and bags of food. He spent the rest of that day learning about the other men and the circumstances that had landed them in jail, in hopes of helping them after his release. The hardest part of all, Huerta said, was when his wife and kids showed up for his arraignment and bail hearing and he had to look them in the eye. He wore a black T-shirt, black jeans, cuffs, and shackles. From his perspective, it was humiliating. From theirs, it was honorable. His sons told me that their dad had held his head high, his shoulders back, and his chest straight. Dozens of other union leaders and workers packed the courtroom to support Huerta, along with Democratic Senator Adam Schiff. Los Angeles Mayor Karen Bass wrote a letter to the judge, urging her to take into account his ties and contributions to the community as she decided bail. Huerta was charged with conspiracy, released on $50,000 bond, and dropped outside without his shoes. Facing a throng of supporters, he defended his actions and spoke of the men he had met who were still stuck inside. “We will have our time for justice,” he told the crowd, “but we must do it in a way that we demonstrate the power of working people.” That night, though, after a long shower and dinner from his favorite taco truck, Huerta lay awake in his bed thinking about a new, terrifying phrase: “conspiracy to impede.” The psychological stress was, Huerta later realized, his worst punishment. He wanted to get back to work, but he didn’t want his presence at the office to draw negative attention to the union. For a time, he worked remotely. Lucía and the boys worried about his health. He had grown moody and snippy, often wanting to be left alone after work. Other family members worried that Huerta’s critics might try to physically harm him. Three attorneys and their teams from Los Angeles and Washington, D.C., represented him pro bono, and nonprofits helped pay other expenses. But Huerta was still stressed about the many hundreds of hours that his case was consuming. All of the time he was spending on his legal fight was time lost organizing laborers, he said. Senator Alex Padilla of California, a Democrat, told me he figured that the federal government’s case against Huerta, a friend, would fail, but he knew it had to be taken seriously. “It was clear to me that it was weighing on him,” Padilla said. But Huerta also understood, the senator said, that his members were “looking to him for how to handle the chaos that was going on and the pain that was being inflicted.” “Free David Huerta” rallies popped up around the state; supporters carried posters featuring his stenciled portrait and wore T-shirts emblazoned with his name. “An injury to one,” the AFL-CIO wrote on Facebook, “is an injury to all.” Huerta attended two rallies to protest Trump’s immigration policies, but kept his appearances short, he told me. He left one as people marched toward a detention facility; he didn’t want to violate the terms of his bond that required he stay at least 100 yards away from federal-law-enforcement agents or operations. One day, while at home with Lucía, he paced the house and imagined how much worse things could get, feeling out of control. “Hey, calm down, you’re okay,” his wife told him. He later realized that he had suffered an anxiety attack. He was raised in a culture in which seeking treatment for mental health carries a stigma, but he knew he needed help. He began to see a therapist for the first time. In October, his charge was downgraded to a misdemeanor. Prosecutors needed to establish that he had planned to interfere with federal officers that day—but his actions were unplanned, his attorneys said. His team tried to get prosecutors to dismiss the case entirely. The lesser charges brought some relief, but his character and reputation were still under attack. “Those anxieties of uncertainty,” he said, “they kept creeping up.” The case continued to weigh heavily on his marriage. Then, in July, prosecutors gave up. Late last month, the charge was dismissed with prejudice. He got back his passport and phone, two items that symbolize his freedom and his work. He did not celebrate. “I was detained—and fortunately, that’s all that happened,” he told me. Others—in Minneapolis, Houston, Maine—had been shot dead while interacting with immigration officers. Bill Essayli, the top federal prosecutor in Los Angeles, said on X that Huerta, since his arrest, had complied with conditions to not interfere with federal agents and operations, but “should he unlawfully interfere with any federal agent engaged in their duties, we will not hesitate to arrest and charge him again.” His office declined further comment. If the point was to try to make an example out of him, a conviction wasn’t necessary. The case against Huerta signaled to the labor and immigrant communities that no one could protest immigration raids without real risk, he and other labor leaders told me. Some union members grew afraid of speaking out, they said. But many others were moved to show their disapproval of the immigration crackdown by protesting peacefully because of how they saw officers treat Huerta, Arnulfo De La Cruz, who took over in April as president of SEIU California, told me. “In a lot of ways, he was the embodiment of a transition from fear to We’re not gonna back down.” Huerta doesn’t know when—or even if—he will show up at another immigration-enforcement protest, he said. His experience has made him think hard about how he tries to achieve his goals. “I’m not leaving the work,” he told me. “But I would be a liar to say that it’s not in the back of my head that I could find myself in the same situation.” On the day I saw him, he had just pulled into town from Big Bear, a rare escape. He spoke not of the views or the lake, but of the workers powering the restaurants and hotels.

This Tiny E-Reader Is the Single Best Piece of Technology in Years
2026-09-06

This Tiny E-Reader Is the Single Best Piece of Technology in Years

The Atlantic | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
No matter how you read it, The Power Broker is a serious undertaking. The paperback weighs as much as a brick; to avoid lugging around all 1,344 pages, people have cut it apart at the spine and chunked it into sections. Others have organized in-person Power Broker marathon readings that run continuously for three full days. I decided to try something else. Over the past several weeks, I’ve spent 10 hours paging through Robert Caro’s masterpiece on an e-reader the size of a credit card. It’s magnetic, and small enough to attach to the back of my iPhone without adding any real bulk to my pocket. (Yes, this does mean that I have a screen on the back of another screen.) It’s called the Xteink X3, and at a glance, it looks like a cheap gimmick: more electronic junk made by unknown brands and sold on Temu and TikTok Shop—the kind of thing influencers are always hawking. (Can I interest you in a programmable LED hat for $11.60? Or how about $20 AirPod look-alikes made by “Fhumsh”?) In a sense, it is that. Xteink sounds less like a real brand name than the kind of garbled text you’ll find in AI-slop memes. (Speaking of which, the official website of the China-based company is full of AI-generated photos of people posing with the device.) And indeed, since the X3’s release on Amazon in May, TikTokers and YouTubers have raved about how the device is a steal and you should buy it right now—just click the link in bio. From our August 2026 issue: The end of reading is here And so I did. On Amazon, the X3 is $69 and tops the “hot new releases” list for e-readers, beating out a slew of Kindles. In person, the Xteink is many things: plasticky. Flawed. Finicky. Nevertheless, it is the single best piece of technology that I have purchased in years. In the two months before getting this cartoonishly tiny e-reader, I finished only one book. In one month with the X3, I read three books without even really trying—and my iPhone’s average daily screen time dipped by 25 minutes. The X3 is great because of its size, not in spite of it. (For once, “fun-size” actually lives up to the billing.) Because this is such a runt of a device, you can stick it to your phone and have an e-reader in your pocket at all times. As a result, I’ve found myself reading on my morning commute, in line at a museum, even while waiting through Netflix ads. The display on my Xteink may be roughly half the size of the smallest Kindle, but the text is big enough to easily read. Even after three straight hours of inching through The Power Broker, my eyes weren’t strained. The device’s size actually helped me develop a different relationship to reading altogether: Because each “page” on the Xteink amounts to just a paragraph or two—and sometimes less—I’m compelled to finish it. And now that I’m onto the next page, I might as well finish that one too. A YouTuber who recently reviewed the Xteink had the same experience—she referred to the uniquely addictive experience of using the device as the “TikTokification of reading.” I know how terrifying that sounds, but consider the outcome: Whenever there’s even a couple of minutes of free time, I now have a healthy alternative to doomscrolling on Instagram. I don’t even have to put my phone down. Flip over the Bad Screen and fire up the Good Screen. For all of its charm, the Xteink is a pretty basic piece of hardware. For $70, you get what you pay for. Other popular e-readers now come with AI-powered recaps, high-definition displays, and Android operating systems. Meanwhile, the Xteink has no touch screen, color display, or backlight for reading in the dark. (Xteink recently launched the X4 Pro, a slightly larger and more expensive model that has a reading light and a touch screen.) Just reading a book on the device isn’t necessarily seamless: The preloaded software is horrific and can’t even display book covers or properly format some text. Unlike with a Kindle, you can’t port over books from Amazon with one click. In spite of itself, the Xteink X3, in all of its 3.7 inches of glory, has become a viral hit. Online, people have posted photos of their Xteink on trips to the Austrian mountains or the Aegean Sea; one anesthesiologist says he tucks it into his scrubs. People are mounting the Xteink X3 to their kitchen hood to display recipes and are making all sorts of bespoke accessories, including 3-D-printed reading lights and a case that looks like a gun (I’m serious). Fans have built their own open-source firmware, Crosspoint, that considerably upgrades the device. The version I downloaded displays reading stats and an array of fonts, and it lets me screenshot pages as I read. Late last month, Crosspoint added the ability to access books on Libby, the popular library ebook app. The best thing about the Xteink is not any one feature. It’s that this tiny e-reader is fun in a way so few gadgets are anymore. Despite all our advances, the consumer-electronics landscape of 2026 is predictable and banal. Our laptops, smartphones, and tablets are thinner and more powerful than ever, but that feels like a weak substitute for innovation. Next week, Apple is poised to launch one of its most hyped products in years: a folding iPhone. But even folding smartphones are old news; Samsung released its version in 2019. After shelling out hundreds or thousands of dollars for a new gadget, you might still have to pay for subscriptions and endure annoying ads. Sometimes, your purchases aren’t even really yours. Amazon recently removed the ability to download ebooks you already own and transfer them to non-Kindle devices. In other words, we need more gadgets like the Xteink: cheap, simple, and customizable. With few exceptions, these products are now coming out of China. Neck fans from brands such as JisuLife and Civpower have become sensations for helping people endure the summer heat. Anker’s portable projectors and earbuds are both Wirecutter-recommended. Silicon Valley has left a gaping hole in the gadgets markets, and Americans are more than willing to look elsewhere.

What’s the Matter With Moderation?
2026-09-06

What’s the Matter With Moderation?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-06

Visit Original Website
One simple rule shapes Kansas politics: Republicans win statewide. In U.S. Senate races, the GOP has prevailed without interruption for more than 90 years. But the rule has a corollary, which accounts for the occasional Democratic governor. A Republican thought to be too extreme, too ideological, or too big for their britches can be beaten by a centrist Democrat when the wind blows just right. And so, on the same August Tuesday that saw Michigan Democrats choose Abdul El-Sayed, Kansas Republicans realized their safe seat might not be so safe. Kansas Democrats had picked a man who extols moderation as a matter of faith—literally. Adam Hamilton, the founding pastor of America’s largest mainline Protestant congregation, will challenge the first-term incumbent Senator Roger Marshall in November. In the handful of months since he announced his candidacy, Hamilton has defeated 10 garden-variety Democrats in the primary while setting new records for Kansas fundraising, pulling in $1 million in his first week and nearly $3.7 million in his first quarter. Political analysts continue to rate Hamilton a long shot; admittedly, they have nine decades of justification. But Marshall ticks every box that can mean trouble for otherwise-invulnerable Kansas Republicans. Full of himself even by senatorial standards (one Republican Senate colleague described him to me as a “know-it-all”), Marshall is leery of public forums where he might hear from protesters and tends to speak before thinking—a hazard of his appetite for cable-TV appearances. In nearly six years as a constituent of the senator, I have traveled from one end of Kansas to the other, and I have concluded that passionate supporters of Roger Marshall are the electoral equivalent of jackalopes: If they exist, I have never found one. Should Hamilton break the Senate’s longest GOP win streak, he would score more than an enormous off-year upset; his influence on 2028—a free-for-all to define post–Donald Trump politics—could be even larger. Because Hamilton seeks to vindicate the belief, beleaguered in today’s culture, that moderation is a good thing, not just as an electoral strategy, but as an approach to life. Elizabeth Bruenig: Texans will decide if Jesus was a lefty In Texas, James Talarico’s Christianity seems, at times, to dovetail with uncanny precision with the agenda of secular progressivism. Hamilton’s rhetoric suggests that his Democratic revival tent would be roomier, but at the same time he has so far faced relatively little pressure to translate his disposition into details. He is motivated by “compassion, a belief in safety nets for vulnerable people,” he told me, and also by “fiscal responsibility, limited government, and strong defense.” Such inoffensive straddling may be out of step with today’s politics. “People are really pissing mad, really, really pissing mad,” El-Sayed explained to Michelle Goldberg of The New York Times. A roiling left has risen to match Trump’s raging right. To win, Hamilton must not only persuade independents and Republicans to vote for him—he must also motivate angry progressives in this time of throat-cutting. If El-Sayed and others like him are the furious faces of the Democratic future, perhaps Hamilton is a DINO. When I asked him if Democrats should seek to impeach Trump a third time, he ventured his belief that “the impeachments in the first term did not accomplish anything”—a claim that dismisses the expression of righteous anger as a political imperative in itself. Hamilton likes to talk about the good that he could accomplish in partnership with the well-liked Senator Jerry Moran, the senior Republican from Kansas. But maybe that’s not what the people, who truly are pissing mad, really want. Emporia is the seat of Lyon County, which in 2024 gave 56 percent of its votes to Donald Trump and 41 percent to Kamala Harris. Yet Hamilton was not in hostile territory when he visited one late-August morning. Gravel City Roasters is a combination coffee shop and art gallery located directly across Emporia’s main drag from the county Democratic headquarters. There is a Pride flag on the wall with Refugees welcome superimposed on its rainbow stripes. The chai, served in mismatched mugs, is excellent. But the comfort zone is narrow here for a 21st-century Democrat. The little city with its college campus is surrounded by farmland that has historically bred beasts, grains, and discontent. In its heyday—the era of William McKinley and Theodore Roosevelt—Emporia was home to the journalist William Allen White, whose Gazette newspaper was a sort of proto-Substack. Readers around the world vibed to his grumbling, which often had to do with the eternal sense of Kansas as undervalued and overlooked. “Go east and you hear them laugh at Kansas; go west and they sneer at her; go south and they cuss her; go north and they have forgotten her,” White wrote in his career-launching screed, dated 1896 and titled “What’s the Matter With Kansas?” Thirteen decades later, an Emporia pharmacist, John Schmidt, picked up the theme, telling me at the coffee shop of the town’s beef processing plant (closed), its Michelin rubber-track factory (downsized), its maker of plastic ice scrapers (victim of a bankrupt holding company), and the nearby drugstore (battered by low reimbursements). The area’s future, too, feels festooned with pink slips. A few dozen people awaited Hamilton, who arrived in a blue blazer and an open-collared white shirt. He immediately went to work shaking hands and nodding thoughtfully. After watching Hamilton campaign half a dozen or so times, the best description I can manage is “pastoral.” Fair enough, I suppose, for a 62-year-old pastor. Hamilton has a soft voice, and his gaze is gentle, intent, and framed by horn-rimmed glasses. His head moves constantly in tiny yeses and no’s, like a seismograph needle registering each quick conversation. After working his way to the rear of the shop—pausing to autograph a woman’s copy of his book on the Holy Spirit—Hamilton used a wireless microphone to tell a joke about hypocritical politicians pretending to know the Lord’s Prayer. “I am a Democrat who actually knows the Lord’s Prayer,” he said. “When it says, ‘give us this day our daily bread,’ it’s reminding us to make sure that people have enough to eat.” A sturdy little stump speech rolled out from there, befitting a man who has been crafting sermons since Seinfeld was new. He said he is a fifth-generation Kansan who married his sweetheart a week after high-school graduation and wants to give his granddaughter “a better country.” He said he built a church from nothing to 24,000 members. He said he prayed a year and a half ago that Trump would govern “with wisdom, grace, and compassion,” but found himself watching something quite different. Somebody’s got to do something became Maybe I should do something. Rather than take on Trump directly, however, he aimed the balance of his fire at Marshall, who, Hamilton noted, “has voted for nearly everything the president has done.” Hamilton said he is against tariffs because they hurt farmers, against the Iran war because it drives up fuel prices, against Medicaid reductions because they threaten rural hospitals, and against cuts to food aid because more than 14,000 Kansas children need that support. “Since when did it become woke to care about your neighbor?” he asked rhetorically, aware that his audience was already seeing attack ads trying to tie him to left-wing ideas, and that Republican Senator Ted Cruz of Texas used an X post to denounce one of Hamilton’s sermons as “heretical.” Don Duncan was part of that audience, having driven to town from nearby Chase County to have a look at the candidate. In the primary, a local Democrat, Christy Davis, drew 113 votes in Chase County, leaving Hamilton with eight; on the Republican side, Marshall got 479. Duncan, a self-described “Democrat who leans independent,” told me he did not think things are as bleak for Hamilton as that would suggest. “It’s just a matter of people getting to know him,” said the retired school administrator and coach. “We need someone to get us back together.” Could that be Marshall? I asked. “I can’t say we ever talk about him,” Duncan answered. About 100 miles north and east, amid the big houses and busy shops of Kansas City’s southern suburbs, stands Hamilton’s monument to open minds, the “church for thinking people” that he founded in 1990. The main sanctuary of Leawood’s United Methodist Church of the Resurrection is visible from all directions, an elliptical edifice clad in glass and stainless steel, vaguely reminiscent of Disney’s Space Mountain. Rising 100 feet above a 77-acre campus of auxiliary buildings and parking lots, it features the largest fused-stained-glass window in the world, a work roughly the size of a basketball court. Nine satellite churches in greater Kansas City—four in Kansas; five in Missouri—carry the COR brand to sites around the area and beyond. If the words megachurch pastor conjure images of hellfire oratory and $400 haircuts, Hamilton comes as a surprise, as mild as Jake from State Farm. Yet his success stands in contrast to the decline of mainstream Christianity in his lifetime. Born in 1964 and raised by divorced parents in Prairie Village, one of those same southern suburbs, Hamilton came of age in a time of Protestant crisis. Perplexed by the social upheaval of the 1960s, Hamilton’s United Methodist denomination lost more than 10 percent of its membership in the 1970s and a further 5 percent in the 1980s. What energy there was in American Christianity came from conservative movements energized by opposition to abortion, homosexuality, scientific modernism, and a grab bag of philosophical attitudes derided by evangelicals as “secular humanism.” Even as a teen, Hamilton found this alienating. Drawn to church for its social possibilities—“I didn’t believe in God, but I believed in girls,” he likes to say—the God he eventually embraced was one who delighted in discovery and gloried in progress. God gave us a brain for a reason, he thought, taking to heart the biblical admonition to worship with all of one’s “heart and soul and mind.” In college at Oral Roberts University in Tulsa, Oklahoma, Hamilton “grew more and more interested in the parts of the Gospel that evangelical churches were leaving behind,” he told me during a July interview at a barbecue joint across the state line in Kansas City, Missouri. “I began focusing on scriptures dealing with social justice, ‘rolling down like waters,’ as Amos has it; the caring for widows and orphans; the story of the Good Samaritan. And I noticed that our churches were great at helping the people who came to church, but not so much with the people who were outside.” Trained as a pastor at Southern Methodist University in Dallas, Hamilton returned with his young family to Kansas City in the late Reagan years. He soon persuaded his regional bishop to let him start a new congregation for people turned off by what Christianity had become. Though United Methodism suffered a further 29 percent drop in membership from 1990 to 2020, Hamilton’s congregation grew from fewer than a dozen people meeting in a funeral-home chapel to the largest mainline Protestant congregation in America. He credits an idea known to theologians as the via media, the “middle way,” which has roots in ancient philosophy and scholastic Christianity, but had its flowering in Elizabethan England. At its core the idea involves a belief that truth is not to be found on the extremes and that compromise in pursuit of worthy aims is a virtue. Society as Hamilton envisions it is a sort of Newton’s cradle, the toy that is composed of a line of metal balls suspended by threads. When the ball on one end is lifted and released, it swings into contact with the pack, where its energy is conducted to the ball on the other extreme, which swings away in reaction. Energy might come from extremism, but stability lies with the center. Peter Wehner: American Christians face a choice COR is that stable center, Hamilton maintains, “roughly equal parts Democrat, Republican, and independent,” where inevitable conflicts among freethinking people can be respected and accommodated in pursuit of shared missions. “I am wired to see both sides,” he told me, “which is not the same as saying that all issues are 50–50. I believe cutting Medicaid and closing rural hospitals is just wrong. Making it harder for public schools to serve special-needs students is wrong, and I am willing to talk with anyone to explain why I think so. But I don’t expect everyone to agree with me all the time.” Hamilton hasn’t shied from hard issues as a pastor. When a faction of American Methodists joined forces with culturally conservative congregations in Africa to try to pass anti-LGBTQ ordinances for the denomination, he led the opposition. Criticizing what he saw as a misguided focus on a few biblical verses over a larger Gospel of inclusion and acceptance, Hamilton ultimately concluded that a schism was inevitable, and he was instrumental in negotiating the many details of separation. The thing to notice about that story, as Hamilton tries to turn from pastor to lawmaker, is that the via media was the road not taken by Hamilton and the Methodists when the chips were down. There was no middle ground, and now there are two denominations where there used to be one. Hamilton would likely run into an even more challenging divide in Washington, where the two factions cannot go their separate ways. Can moderation really be an agenda? In the biblical story of King Solomon, when confronted with a seemingly unresolvable impasse, the wise solution was not to split the baby, but for one combatant to surrender for the greater good. That’s not the Washington way. Electoral politics is, in some ways, just second-grade math: addition and subtraction. Candidates seek to add votes in Column A while subtracting votes from Column B. All of the money, overt or dark; all of the advertisements; all of the pandering and polarizing; all of the door-knocking, the text messages, the awkward TikToks, the yard signs, the stilted visits by candidates to factories, diners, fairgrounds, living rooms, nursing homes, colleges—it’s all about addition and subtraction. For Hamilton, the problem pencils out at around 80,000 votes. Any combination of addition and subtraction that hits this target, whether raising the Democratic vote share or reducing Marshall’s compared with his 2020 victory, will get the job done. That’s a big number in a state the size of Kansas, where about 1.3 million people voted in 2024, but so is the number of people who cram the pews of Hamilton’s sanctuaries each Christmas and Easter: around 100,000 per holiday. As shown by the August 4 results, his stronghold is in Johnson County, home to his church and to about a quarter of the state’s voters. Once a bastion of chamber-of-commerce Republicanism, the county turned purple during its explosive growth in the Baby Boom years and has, in the Trump era, become a relatively safe base for Democratic Representative Sharice Davids. Driving up turnout in Johnson County and other metro centers—working-class Wyandotte County (Kansas City, Kansas), liberal Douglas County (Lawrence, home to the University of Kansas), booming Sedgwick County (Wichita, still the state’s largest city)—will be the keys to Hamilton’s addition problem. As for subtraction, meet Marshall. A retired ob-gyn who calls himself “Doc”—a nickname that hasn’t caught on with Kansas voters—he has distinguished himself as one of Trump’s most obsequious acolytes, though not always the most on-message. Marshall’s ready-fire-aim approach to public speaking has left his candidacy riddled with self-inflicted wounds. Asked whether he’s okay with Iran having ballistic missiles (the program Trump was promising to destroy), Marshall said, “I would prefer they didn’t. But I don’t think that’s the key issue here. I think that they have to be able to defend themselves.” Of rising gas prices triggered by the war: “Freedom is not free. Americans are going to have to make some sacrifices.” When Kansans died on a plane that collided with an Army helicopter over Washington, Marshall blithely blamed their deaths on DEI, a theory unsupported by crash investigators. Having sold his house in central Kansas shortly after being elected to the Senate, Marshall is dogged by allegations that his status as a Kansas resident is largely fictitious. He lists as his residence a roughly 1,200-square-foot cabin on a family ranch property, but for several years his congressionally paid travel plans included frequent stops at his “vacation” home in Florida, which he says has been uninhabitable since suffering severe hurricane damage in 2024. Marshall’s campaign did not respond to my requests to discuss the race for this article, but in a recent social-media post he wrote, “Over the last six years, I’ve averaged 185 days per year in D.C. doing the work you sent me to do, 130 days per year in Kansas with over 300 stops per year across the state, about 35 days of official travel, and 20 holiday/personal days.” When he does visit Kansas, Marshall has avoided open meetings with the public ever since a crowd in a rural community jeered him in March 2025 during Elon Musk’s DOGE fiasco. “The town halls just became non-productive and dangerous,” Marshall told a newspaper in Hays, Kansas. The odor of weakness around the incumbent helped inspire the GOP-dominated legislature this year to spitball easing Marshall out of office in favor of an appointed successor whose term they would extend through 2028. Voilà! No election needed. The scheme died amid criticism that the Constitution doesn’t allow states to change the length of a Senate term, no matter how lame their senator might be. With no choice but to defend Marshall, the GOP is probing for Hamilton’s vulnerabilities. A Massachusetts-based super PAC spent a reported $600,000 on a TV ad that pieces together phrases from sermons to portray Hamilton as “mega-woke.” “Black Lives Matter,” Hamilton says in the ad, “marching for righteousness.” In another moment, marked with an ellipsis on-screen, he says, “By virtue of being white … I have to repent.” The Kansas City Star dug up the June 2020 sermon from which the clip is taken and found Hamilton saying “I have certain benefits by virtue of being educated, white, and male, right?” adding, almost a minute later, “Before I can do anything to help anyone else, I have to have a conversion. I have to repent.” Ted Cruz’s X post avowing that Hamilton held “heretical” views relied on a sermon in which Hamilton says that non-Christians might enter heaven. Marshall thanked Cruz in his own post and added, “Wait until you hear about the ‘hundreds of mistakes in the Bible’ my opponent claims to have found.” In response, the Hamilton campaign shared a quote from Joshua Leu, a pastor of a church in Great Bend, Kansas, where Marshall once served on the board. HuffPost had asked Leu about Hamilton. “I would say that he’s a middle-of-the-road pastor who is willing to listen to all sides, and then based on his understanding of the gospel, try to make the best decision,” Leu said. Attacking Hamilton as a fellow traveler of the progressive left, a democratic socialist in gossamer disguise, might work in Kansas. But there are reasons why even Kansans who reject that portrait as grossly inaccurate might still say no to his candidacy. The party of Lincoln still has deep resonance in a state where people were battling years before Fort Sumter. Bob Dole’s success at amassing power in Washington and using it to funnel money to the state taught lessons in the value of seniority that are still remembered. Western Kansas is not only overwhelmingly Republican, but it is also suspicious of what it regards as the citified East, where Hamilton is from. And not even all of his Johnson County congregation’s members are Hamilton supporters, by the candidate’s own assessment. As the church has grown, some members have griped that Hamilton has become less accessible—an issue that his Senate campaign is likely to exacerbate, to say nothing of actually serving in the Senate. He’s taken a leave from sermonizing for now, but would stay on as senior pastor and preach at least a dozen times a year if elected. A Kansas Democrat starts out in such a deep hole in a Senate race that Hamilton considered running as an independent, but the recent example of the failed independent Greg Orman (who lost a race for Senate in 2014 and governor in 2018) convinced the pastor and his advisers that only a Democrat could unseat Marshall. When voters ask, as they frequently do, how Hamilton would deal with pressure from party leaders, he told me his answer is: “I won’t work for Chuck Schumer; I’ll work for the people of Kansas.” But other candidates in the region—Dan Osborn in Nebraska, Brian Bengs in South Dakota—have chosen the middlest of ways by running as independents. Perhaps Hamilton’s decision to step off the via media by allying himself with one of our nation’s two political parties is a concession to the reality of politics. Before Hamilton can be as nettlesome for Schumer as, say, West Virginia’s Joe Manchin was during the administration of Joe Biden, he first must win in November. Well-intentioned concessions, however, are the beginning of another, much more famous, road. With money continuing to pour in from more than 16,000 donors, Hamilton can answer the attacks. And he has another shield in his armory. A prolific author of books on scripture, prayer, and Seeing Gray in a World of Black and White, Hamilton has created texts and instructional videos that are staples at Bible studies and Sunday-school classes in at least 500 Kansas churches, he told me. This makes him something of a known commodity in rural communities that might otherwise be leery of a big-city candidate. As he calculates, 500 churches averaging 50 people each represents a small army of 25,000 people at least partly inoculated against anti-Hamilton attack ads. He doesn’t expect to win them all in counties where Democrats sometimes number in single digits, and he doesn’t need to. Maybe a handful in each precinct, in the secrecy of the voting booth, stung by the price of tractor fuel and fertilizer, will mark his name, or simply skip Marshall’s. Hamilton is not a dynamic political performer; he doesn’t suck the oxygen from the room. The son of a service-station manager, a husband and father by the end of his teens, Hamilton instead relies on approachability, on his feeling for life as ordinary people live it: sometimes messy; sometimes painful. He seems to genuinely believe that what people have in common matters more than their differences. You can build a church on that. But can you build a campaign? Molly Worthen: What the fastest-growing Christian group reveals about America Jim Hightower, the former Texas agriculture commissioner turned liberal pundit, used to say, “There is nothing in the middle of the road but yellow stripes and dead armadillos.” But Kansas has—or had—something of a tradition of moderate Republican senators in the not-so-distant past. Nancy Kassebaum, the daughter of former Governor Alf Landon, the Republican Party’s 1936 presidential nominee (even Kansas went for Franklin D. Roosevelt that year), died last month. She served three terms in the Senate, from 1978 to 1997. She was a Republican who worked on health care, voted to ban assault weapons, and opposed, for nearly all of her career, a constitutional amendment to balance the budget. “We should be wary of the seemingly simple solution,” she said. Hamilton might anger partisans on the left and right—in his pews and beyond—by adhering to the middle way. He will need to make compromises in order to win, or, should he win, in order to govern. Politics inevitably sullies religion. But Jesus, in the Gospel of Matthew, also recommends the narrow road instead of the wide and easy one. As a student of the via media, Hamilton surely knows what came for Queen Elizabeth’s moderate vision. The elegant span she championed connecting Catholic structures and rituals to Protestant individualism proved a bridge to nowhere. The decades after her death belonged to the extremists. Rivers of blood flowed across Europe as Catholics and Protestants waged their Thirty Years’ War. In England, sectarian Puritans staged a regicidal revolution. Refugees from religious violence poured into the Americas. Some eras in history are like that, obstreperous and irreconcilable. Hamilton likes to point out that Kansas contains the geographical center of the lower 48 states, and he hopes to find America’s political midpoint too. The risk is that, like so much of the Farm Belt, the center has been hollowed out.

[cbsnews.com]         "The U.S. job market is less robust than it looks, according to a new analysis.         The nation's unemployment rate fell to 4.1% in July, well within the range economists consider healthy. Yet that measure, which reflects the percentage of people 16 and older who don't have a job and who are looking for work, is an imprecise metric that fails to offer a full picture of the health of the job sector, according to Gene Ludwig, chairman of the Ludwig Institute for Shared Economic Prosperity (LISEP).         The economic research firm's "True Rate of Unemployment" (TRU) seeks to capture the share of workers that the group defines as "functionally unemployed." This includes not only people who are unemployed and looking for a job, but also those who are involuntarily working part-time and who are earning poverty-level wages, or less than $26,000 annually before taxes.         Functional unemployment across the U.S. has climbed four straight months and stood at 24.9% as of July, although that's down from 25.2% in December, according to the Ludwig Institute. ..." [shadowstats.com] To me, these sorts of huge (but often ignored) unemployment numbers reflect the continued playing out of larger trends like I outlined in 2010:"Beyond a Jobless Recovery: A heterodox perspective on 21st century economics"https://pdfernhout.net/beyond-... [pdfernhout.net]         "This article explores the issue of a "Jobless Recovery" mainly from a heterodox economic perspective. It emphasizes the implications of ideas by Marshall Brain and others that improvements in robotics, automation, design, and voluntary social networks are fundamentally changing the structure of the economic landscape. It outlines towards the end four major alternatives to mainstream economic practice (a basic income, a gift economy, stronger local subsistence economies, and resource-based planning). These alternatives could be used in combination to address what, even as far back as 1964, has been described as a breaking "income-through-jobs link". This link between jobs and income is breaking because of the declining value of most paid human labor relative to capital investments in automation and better design. Or, as is now the case, the value of paid human labor like at some newspapers or universities is also declining relative to the output of voluntary social networks such as for digital content production (like represented by this document). It is suggested that we will need to fundamentally reevaluate our economic theories and practices to adjust to these new realities emerging from exponential trends in technology and society. ..." The part that bothers me here isn't that a Carrington-scale storm could knock out the grid. We've known that for a long time. It's that we seem to be counting on "restoration" as though replacing a damaged transformer is roughly equivalent to replacing a server. It isn't. Some of these things are enormous, expensive, custom-built pieces of equipment with long lead times. If enough of them are damaged at once, there may not be a lot of spare capacity sitting around waiting to be plugged in. And the interconnection issue is worth thinking about. We have spent decades making the grid more interconnected because that's generally efficient and useful. But a system that is wonderfully efficient under normal conditions isn't necessarily resilient when the disturbance is spread across an entire region. If Maine and Washington DC are ultimately part of the same electrical machine, "just isolate the problem" sounds a lot easier on paper than it might be at 2 AM during a once-in-a-century event. What makes this particularly annoying is that this isn't some exotic science-fiction threat. We can watch the Sun. We know roughly what kind of event we're worried about. We know which parts of the grid are vulnerable. And we know that having some additional transformers, better monitoring, better procedures and a real emergency plan would cost money, but probably far less money than discovering after the fact that we didn't have any of those things. You don't buy a fire extinguisher because you know the date of the fire. The reason we're counting on restoration is because the design of these systems protect critical equipment like transformers. We calculate fault levels, speed of rise levels and design systems to interrupt and protect critical infrastructure (at the expense of the customer's connected). You're not going to be replacing large transformers. You're going to be replacing small scale control electronics in the substation. You're going to be replacing countless single action fuses.
