What Was in the Mind of the Holdout Juror in the Lindsay Clancy Trial? I Can Certainly Speculate.
2026-09-05

What Was in the Mind of the Holdout Juror in the Lindsay Clancy Trial? I Can Certainly Speculate.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. On Friday, the judge in the Lindsay Clancy case declared a mistrial after a week of jury deadlock. Clancy is charged with murdering her three children in what prosecutors say was a cold-blooded killing, and in what she says was a fit of psychosis after months spent seeking help and being put on a long list of drugs for her declining mental health. One juror was the holdout, refusing to vote for acquittal. He reportedly recognized that there was reasonable doubt as to her responsibility for her crime, but would not apply the appropriate legal standard. The disarray and unsettledness that characterizes the end of this trial is unsatisfying but also in line with the whole of the Clancy saga. The story is a tragic one: Three innocent children dead, a family in shambles. And it’s also been a galvanizing one, as women the nation over have spoken out in Clancy’s defense, noting their own postpartum struggles; many have shown up at her trial to support her, clad in pink. And there has of course been a backlash, albeit a smaller one, of people who insist that a mother who kills her children must simply be evil, no other explanation necessary (or even possible). It’s hard to know what was in the mind of the lone holdout juror, the one who wanted to find Clancy guilty of being in her right mind when she killed her children even as he realized the prosecution has not proven its case. I can certainly speculate: Some people want a simple story of good and evil. Some people need a punitive outcome to a horrific event, no matter actual fault or responsibility. Some people just want women punished. Anecdotally, I’ve noticed a sharp gender divide in opinion on the Clancy case (and something of a political divide, too, though a far less pronounced one). The people demanding Clancy go to prison are overwhelmingly conservative men. Women, and especially mothers, are not cheering Clancy on, but are largely calling for some nuance and care. Not every mother struggles in the postpartum period. But many of us—most of us?—have experienced the brain-and-body altering effects of pregnancy, childbirth, and childrearing: Have felt our post-birth hormones crash, sending us reeling into despair; have felt bobble-headed and nearly insane after weeks or months of sleepless, cry-pierced nights, pulling from some reservoir of human survival we didn’t know existed within us to meet seemingly endless needs; have been pushed into unhinged rage and then profound shame by a defiant toddler. And then if you have more than one child, you may well be managing that defiant toddler while simultaneously experiencing the hormone crash, the sleeplessness, the bobble-headed feeling of the newborn period all over again. Most women are not saying “I understand why Lindsay Clancy killed her kids.” But I suspect that most of us know we don’t understand only by the grace of God or luck. Motherhood has taken many of us to new emotional lands—sometimes soaring heights, but sometimes far down into dark depths. Postpartum psychosis, as Clancy says she experienced, is rare. But stunningly high numbers of women report having intrusive thoughts about harming their children—moments when their own brains betray them, when it’s clear that none of us are in total control over our own minds. According to one study, a new mom is just as likely to have disturbing thoughts of intentionally harming her child as she is to not have such thoughts. The women who have these thoughts are not bad or evil. They might, in fact, be excellent, caring mothers. But depending on the severity of the thoughts and their regularity, they may need help—which in the US, they might have a very hard time getting. Much has been written about the women who support Clancy, including those who have descended into conspiracy theorizing. Less attention has been paid to those who are convinced that a suburban mom who was by all accounts deeply loving decided one day to kill her three kids, slash her own neck, and then climb out of a window, leaving herself paralyzed. The whole theory seems to be that she was just a selfish bitch who no longer wanted to be a mom. But that doesn’t quite make sense given her nearly-successful suicide. I suspect, instead, that many people—especially people who have not borne children, especially people who put much stock in traditional gender roles, and especially people who take a more simplistic black-and-white worldview—simply cannot wrap their minds around the fact that sometimes, even good mothers can crack up with the most unthinkably awful results. If your worldview separates women out into “good” and “bad,” with good women pursuing the natural and God-given path of marrying and procreating and dedicating their selfless lives to their children, and bad women working against nature to pursue their own interests or not put their children first, then a mother like Lindsay Clancy is especially destabilizing. She was squarely on the “good woman” side, and then she committed the worst imaginable crime. She always aspired to have lots of kids, her own mother testified, and even her career, as a labor and delivery nurse, was centered around bringing children into the world. The only explanation for the horrific turn her life took must be that she was secretly evil all along. To believe that even good women might see their minds betray them so spectacularly would be to rescind control over an entire ideology that helps organize your world. There are also people who I suspect simply get stuck in the impossible space of wanting justice for three innocent children and finding no clear path to get it. “Justice,” in the parlance of the American legal system and true crime podcasts, generally means punishment, which generally means prison. How might justice be done for Cora, Dawson, and Callan if the person who killed them doesn’t spend the rest of her life behind bars? That she may have committed the crime but may not be actually responsible for it leaves an uncomfortable vacuum, with no sense of finality. The realities of our legal system do not make this any easier. The jury in the Clancy case could have found her guilty and sent her to prison for the rest of her life, which may feel like a fair result for a person who killed three children but is a profoundly unsatisfying and unjust one if the person in question was in the throes of psychosis and not legally responsible for her actions. The jury could have acquited her by reason of insanity, which would then send her to a mental institution, the duration of her stay determined by doctors. That, too, may feel inadequate and potentially perilous—would Clancy be freed in just a few years? Could she go on to have other children? Compassion offers little sense of closure. We don’t know what was motivating the solo juror who wanted to send Clancy to jail rather than to a psychiatric hospital. We don’t know if the prosecution will re-try Clancy, or what will happen next. We do know that scores of mothers saw Lindsay Clancy and felt a twinge of understanding. We know that some of those mothers are still howling for help. We know there are scores of others, mostly men, who seem invested in writing those women off as crazy or unnatural or evil. We know this trial may have “raised awareness” about postpartum mental health, but has not actually changed any tangible aspect of support for women struggling with it. Ultimately, what this trial has revealed is just as incomplete and unsatisfying as its conclusion. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Republicans’ Plan for the Midterms: Bet on Trump Caring About Someone Other Than Himself
2026-09-05

Republicans’ Plan for the Midterms: Bet on Trump Caring About Someone Other Than Himself

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-05

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Surge, the newsletter that covers the most important political nonsense of the week, delivered to your inbox every Saturday. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that has no time for a joke right here because we have important business to discuss. Starting next week, the Surge is going to look different: sleeker and fresher but still phenomenally trenchant. Alongside a refreshed design, the Surge will break down the news of the week into three categories: What you need to pay attention to now, what you need to keep an eye on, and which story is a bunch of nonsense you should ignore. Also, starting in October, the Surge will be available exclusively to Slate Plus subscribers. If you would like to subscribe to Slate Plus and the many riches it offers, use promo code SURGE25 at checkout for a 25 percent discount on a full-year subscription. We appreciate you, Surge Nation. Anyway, enough with the business talk. Let’s chat about something fun, like American politics, and its most important feature: money. MAGA Inc. is the president’s super PAC. As of July 31, it had $403 million on hand. The Trump associates who run it have kept telling and telling and telling House and Senate members that the balance will be spent on their races this fall, a cash bomb to help save GOP majorities in Congress. But it is now September of an election year, and the super PAC has thrown only a few bucks here and there. Where’s the cash? The Atlantic reported last week that Donald Trump was planning to spend soon, but with a twist: He had ordered “a series of television advertisements about his own record that will serve as a backdrop to the hundreds of individual House and Senate contests.” In other words, he called for ads celebrating himself. As you can guess, given Trump’s yawning unpopularity, this idea is going over poorly with the traditional GOP consultant class. Beyond that, will MAGA Inc. unload in individual races, mentioning—or attacking—individual candidates? Given that it is, again, September, Republicans are starting to doubt they’ll see much of this money. As the Washington Post reported, GOP leaders have begun to “conclude that it’s too late to fully deploy the cash in the midterms and that he intends to hold on to much of it for his own purposes.” This, dear reader. This is … it’s perfect, isn’t it? The single candidate most in need of MAGA Inc.’s largesse is Texas Senate candidate Ken Paxton. Given all of his scandals, baggage, and poor personal fundraising abilities, Paxton could use a heavy infusion of cash to defeat Democratic candidate James Talarico in a large, expensive state with umpteen media markets. But ever since Trump endorsed Paxton over Sen. John Cornyn (who had a much easier, and cheaper, path to victory) in the primary, Senate Republicans and big Texas donors have been of the mind that it’s Trump’s responsibility—not theirs—to fund his campaign. And so we have a bit of a standoff here. A Texas-size standoff. (Sorry, couldn’t resist. Last week of Surge 1.0!) The campaign to push MAGA Inc. to get involved there showed signs of urgency this week. Senate Majority Leader John Thune said on Fox News that “we’re going to need the president and his team” to get involved. Louisiana Sen. John Kennedy said he was “hoping that the president’s going to spend $100 or $200 million in Texas.” And the dean of Texas strategists, Karl Rove, warned that if Trump doesn’t get involved soon, “Talarico is going to run the message of ‘My opponent is the most corrupt politician in Texas history’ all the way to the victory lane.” All well and good. But have any of these complainers considered some of the nice golden trims Trump could use for his presidential library if he keeps this money? Let’s get priorities straight here. Here’s when we’ll know that artificial intelligence has achieved superintelligence: when we tell ChatGPT “Come up with a political event that the Surge could not possibly look forward to less” and it responds “The Republican midterm convention in Dallas.” (So far, the cowardly robot refuses to “invent one.”) Alas, the convention is happening next week for two days, and our dim view of the whole shebang appears to be widely shared within the GOP. Politico surveyed 70 GOP candidates and members and found that most of them weren’t going because they had convenient scheduling conflicts. As for the programming, the GOP intends to highlight its work on tax cuts and border security, as well as its support for things like stock trading bans for members of Congress. (There is no apparent programming for anti-corruption measures within the West Wing.) Now, there is a world where this could be productive for Republicans if they stuck to the script. But Trump, who was at first scheduled to speak only the second night, has now decided that he will speak both nights, ensuring that his showmanship will dominate all coverage. The good news for the GOP, at least, is that Trump’s speeches on both nights will be drowned out by the first two NFL games of the season. In the Massachusetts Senate primary on Tuesday, 80-year-old Sen. Ed Markey, who was first sent to Washington the year Jimmy Carter won the presidency, handily defeated a primary challenge from Rep. Seth Moulton. Our colleague Ian Prasad Philbrick wrote an excellent breakdown of the result this week. In a state like Massachusetts, where the Democratic campaign strategy is simple—run your ass to the left and never look back—Markey had the progressive bona fides to survive Democratic primary voters’ War on Old Incumbents. Moulton, meanwhile, had coded himself as unforgivably centrist, if not viciously right-wing, by saying, after the 2024 election, that he didn’t believe that trans women should play in women’s sports. This marks the second consecutive time Old Man Markey has fended off a former rising star in the party, having tanked Joe Kennedy III’s challenge in 2020. We would find it unusual if Markey ran again in 2032. If he does, though, good luck to whichever next-big-thing in Massachusetts politics chooses to end their career by primarying him. Two House Democrats—Washington Rep. Marie Gluesenkamp Perez and Maine Rep. Jared Golden—broke ranks with their party this week on a procedural vote teeing up votes on several bills, allowing the procedural vote to pass. The reason they did so was to ensure they got a vote on a bill that would help lobstermen. But their procedural votes also set up a vote on a messaging bill from Republicans that both condemned socialism “in all its forms” (lol) and called for the enactment of the SAVE America Act, Trump and Republicans’ hard-line elections bill. It’s a typical election-year poison-pill resolution that Republicans can use to run campaign ads saying Democrats voted to support socialism. Democratic members in competitive races did not want to vote on it but then had to, thanks to their friends from Washington and Maine. House Democrats were furious, and sass was thrown around freely. House Democratic Leader Hakeem Jeffries said he didn’t get a heads-up from the members, telling reporters, “You’ll have to talk to Katherine”—as in Rep. Katherine Clark, the whip. Rep. Nancy Pelosi, against whom Jeffries will always be measured, said that when she was in charge, “we didn’t even consider that anybody would do that.” Rep. Jim McGovern, the top Democrat on the Rules Committee, turned his back to Gluesenkamp Perez while she testified in the committee the following day. Members are so angry that the party is considering punishments for the members—Golden will be leaving Congress in a hot minute and got his lobster bill passed, so he couldn’t care less—and new caucus rules to ensure that such things don’t happen again. That might be a little heavy-handed when a simple talking-to, or a vote-whipping operation that’s more on top of things, could suffice. Last week, we wrote about Pete Hegseth’s firing of Gen. Randy George, the Army chief of staff, over a repeated series of clashes, including the question of whether a military helicopter fly-by at Kid Rock’s house was worthy of investigation. This week, Army Secretary Dan Driscoll submitted his resignation, a move that “had been seen as increasingly imminent after months of friction with Hegseth and others aligned with the Pentagon chief,” per the Post. What’s interesting about this one is that Driscoll has friends in places above Hegseth’s pay grade. He’s friends with Vice President J.D. Vance, with whom he attended Yale Law School. And when Driscoll personally brought his concerns about Hegseth’s leadership to Trump last week, Trump both encouraged Driscoll to stay on and reportedly “expressed concern about the deep cuts to the Army’s senior leadership.” Could this be the beginning of Hegseth losing favor with Trump? We’re going to say … no, Trump probably forgot about it in five minutes, when he resumed thinking about marble samples for the ballroom. Good news: These stupid midterms will be over soon, and then we can turn to the stupid presidential race for our yuks. On the GOP side, don’t expect it to simply be a race between Secretary of State Marco Rubio and Vance. Among others, Sen. Ted Cruz, who has waited three long cycles to give it another go after placing second to Trump in 2016, is making moves. As the Wall Street Journal reported from Iowa this week, Cruz has been “quietly asking people if they would be interested in working on his 2028 bid.” For some time now, too, he’s been pushing back on elements of MAGA, such as the growing antisemitism in the Tucker Carlson wing of the far right and overreaches by the Federal Communications Commission. In other words, he’s trying to carve a lane for himself as the representative of pre-Trump conservatism. Such a strategy depends on there being a backlash within the GOP to Trump-style populism, as well as general sloppiness as his presidency wanes. All we can say is, uh, good luck, we guess? The main reason we’re posting this is because Vance was asked about a Cruz 2028 run on Thursday and had, essentially, the same response as us: “And on Ted Cruz, I mean, look, man, he’s just clearly—Ted is a very authentic and likable guy, and I’m sure he’ll do great.” A deluge of cattiness awaits us in 2027. It’s past time to say it: This Supreme Court is helping President Donald J. Trump evade accountability. Erwin Chemerinsky, dean of the Berkeley School of Law, joins Dahlia Lithwick to break down the conservative majority’s procedural toolkit—standing, ripeness, the Purcell principle, and inscrutable rulings on the shadow docket—and how these doctrinal headfakes are being used to ensure Trump always wins in court, even when he loses. Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.
2026-09-04

Can You Really Get Fired From Fox News for Being Too Trumpy? I’m Skeptical.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Maria Bartiromo, the conservative TV host known for her staunch support of Donald Trump, is out at Fox News. Generally speaking, we at the Slatest try to avoid getting wrapped up in palace intrigue, especially media palace intrigue. But this particular ouster offers an important window into how things really work at Fox—as well as into the broader conservative media ecosystem in which the network is the apex predator. Details are still trickling out, but here’s what we know. On Thursday, Fox announced that Bartiromo was “no longer with” the network, effective immediately. It didn’t give a reason, but reporters quickly put the pieces together. The drama seems to have started in July, when a Fox executive told the network’s on-air personalities to shy away from talking about a major Trump speech about election security. In that primetime address, the president floated conspiratorial insinuations about other countries interfering in the 2020 election, which he claims was stolen from him. Bartiromo, who has covered Trump’s election lies before, apparently forwarded the Fox executive’s guidance to the White House—“as if to say it wasn’t her fault that the claims weren’t getting airtime,” CNN reports—and Fox reportedly labeled Bartiromo’s disclosure a violation of her employment agreement. She’s contesting the emerging narrative—and maybe her apparent ouster, too. “The irresponsible reports that have been published stating that Maria Bartiromo was fired or is no longer an employee of Fox are absolutely and unequivocally false,” Bartiromo’s lawyer said today. “Make no mistake, we have the receipts and witnesses and they will come out, whether through the courthouse or otherwise.” Spicy! But assuming the reporting is right, is Bartiromo really the first Fox employee to get too close to the White House? No. During his first term, the president reportedly consulted the network’s on-air personalities for advice, sometimes even putting them on speakerphone in the Oval Office. One regular confidant was Sean Hannity, who even appeared briefly at a campaign rally with the president. Yet Hannity remains a Fox News host in good standing, and the network’s airwaves have become even more of a revolving door since Trump retook power. Several top officials this term—including Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and Jeanine Pirro, the U.S. attorney for the District of Columbia—were previously Fox personalities. Nor does Bartiromo seem like the only Fox insider who’s passed along sensitive internal info. Roger Ailes, the network’s former CEO, reportedly tipped off Trump’s 2016 campaign about a question later posed by a Fox reporter, and Hannity even hid the fact that he’d apparently shared a lawyer with the president (Michael Cohen, who’s now back in Trump’s good graces). Hang on: Fox (reportedly) fired Bartiromo! So doesn’t that prove the network isn’t just an arm of the White House? Trump’s 2020 election lies are among his most consistently expressed beliefs. Declining to give them airtime—and cutting ties with a host who has promoted them—certainly looks like an act of courage. But it has to be considered in the context of a network that constantly promotes many of Trump’s other lies. And, as noted, network personnel have been caught colluding with him. So what’s different here? There may be a baser motive at play: money. Bartiromo’s zealous coverage of the president’s claims had become a legal headache, helping cost Fox hundreds of millions of dollars in one defamation suit. Oh yeah. Remind me what happened in that case? In 2021, Dominion Voting Systems sued Fox for airing unsubstantiated allegations—including on Bartiromo’s show—that the company’s ballot-tabulation machines were rigged to steal the election. Just as the case was about to go to trial, Fox settled for $787 million. Fox is also facing a similar suit from another voting machine company, Smartmatic, in which Bartiromo is a defendant. According to NPR, Fox’s lawyers went to court for a hearing in that case on Thursday—the same day the network announced Bartiromo’s departure. (Bartiromo’s wouldn’t be the first Trump-friendly head to roll over the president’s election lies. The network ousted longtime host Lou Dobbs after Smartmatic sued and parted ways with Tucker Carlson, who also aired Trump’s false claims, days after the Dominion settlement.) Ouch. But what does this episode tell us about conservative media in general? It leads to some pretty cynical conclusions about the Murdochs, the billionaire family that owns Fox News, the New York Post, and other organs of the right-wing information ecosystem. Rupert Murdoch, who officially handed off his media empire to his son Lachlan last year, arguably helped create the political substrate that germinated Trump, who regularly appeared on Fox to criticize Barack Obama years before running for president. The Trump era has also been good for the Murdochs’ properties. Fox remains the country’s most-watched cable news network, and its other hosts regularly carry water for Trump’s agenda. So rather than taking a stand for the truth or against the president, Fox seems to have ditched Bartiromo to save face—and maybe cash. Trump isn’t pleased; “her fans, of which there are many, will not be happy,” he wrote in a valedictory Truth Social post. Yet Bartiromo’s ouster seems unlikely to break the symbiotic relationship that continues to benefit both the president and his favorite network. In response to questions about why she was let go, Fox called it “a business decision.” Indeed. It’s the holiday weekend, so you, like Bartiromo, likely have some extra free time on your hands. My colleagues have some great suggestions for how to spend it. Go to the movies: If you’re planning a trip to the theater this weekend, allow us to offer some suggestions. If you want to watch a comedy, check out Coyote vs. Acme. If you’re looking for action and adventure, Sam calls The End of Oak Street a “nasty little throwback to Jurassic Park.” For horror fans, It Ends will shake you to your core. If you’re in the mood for a coming-of-age drama, the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony is “is a perfect little movie,” according to Dana Stevens. We would recommend skipping The Dog Stars and The Whisper Man, which were both disappointing adaptations of great books. Binge a new TV show: If you’d rather stay in and stream something, we have a couple of recommendations. Sterling Point is a summer romance series that “will stand the test of time,” according to Rebecca Onion. Rebecca also raves about the crime thriller Furious, which features one of the best performances on TV right now. Lanterns, writes Sam Adams, “is a superhero show for people who think they don’t like superhero shows.” Sam was also pleasantly surprised by the newest season of Ted Lasso, which bounced back after its previous dreadful season. Finally, if you are craving some juicy reality TV, Scaachi Koul dubbed TLC’s Double Lives of Suburban Wives, which follows a group of Christian moms doing sex work, an “absolute banger.” Celebrate Labor Day with a new tradition: Sorry to Bother You, Boots Riley’s 2018 film starring LaKeith Stanfield, is a rollicking sci-fi black comedy about a dystopian workplace. It is also, argues Terri Gerstein, the perfect movie to rewatch this weekend. She argues that what It’s a Wonderful Life is to Christmas and Groundhog Day is to Groundhog Day, Sorry to Bother You should be to Labor Day. Sample some new snacks: Summer barbecues are rife with potato-chip mediocrity. This weekend, leave the predictable bag of chips behind and win the cookout with thrilling bites from Thailand, Canada, and even … Pennsylvania. Danny Palumbo recommends the best snacks to enliven your cookout. Or, if you prefer softer spuds, Danny also has a genius tip for leveling up your potato salad. As the last thing standing between you and a long weekend, let me get out of your way. We’re taking Labor Day off, so I’ll be back in your inbox on Tuesday. ’Til then! The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?
2026-09-04

The Lindsay Clancy Case Has Ended In a Mistrial. What Happens Now?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. The case of Lindsay Clancy has officially ended in a mistrial. The high-profile murder case has consumed national media—for better and for worse—for weeks now, as a jury considered whether the Massachusetts mother intentionally took the lives of her three children or if her crime (and punishment) could be mitigated by reason of insanity. After the jury spent nearly 40 hours deliberating, the judge declared a mistrial on Friday afternoon. That means this case is far from over, with plenty of options, and questions, remaining about Clancy’s fate—and if and how the commonwealth of Massachusetts will try her again. For some broader context: Clancy’s case was exceptionally rare, as she pleaded guilty to strangling her three children back in 2023 but argued that she should not be held criminally liable because she was suffering from postpartum psychosis at the time. The prosecution argued that she was able to parse right from wrong and preplanned to commit the atrocities at the Clancy family home. The jury, made up of nine women and three men, spent six days deliberating the case and twice sent notes to the judge indicating they could not reach a unanimous verdict. Kevin Reddington, Clancy’s defense attorney, suggested that a sole juror had admitted to feeling there was reasonable doubt concerning her guilt but refused to join the 11 jurors who were ready to find Clancy not guilty, per her insanity defense. On Friday, as Judge William Sullivan was announcing a mistrial, Reddington asked to appeal his decision with the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court. Sullivan obliged, allowing Reddington one hour to file his appeal, but it ultimately failed, with the state Supreme Court quickly holding a hearing and denying the application for an emergency stay. To unpack the other complicated details of this case’s non-ending, I spoke with Effie Blassberger, a trial attorney at Clayman Rosenberg Kirshner & Linder who focuses on #MeToo–related litigation alongside white-collar criminal defense. Here is our conversation, which has been edited and condensed for clarity. Shirin Ali: The jury’s landing point in this case was described by Clancy’s attorney as an “intractable position.” What does that mean, specifically within the context of reasonable doubt? Effie Blassberger: It’s basically saying that one juror has a fixed view and it’s not moving. The judge sent the jury back to deliberate several times, and the idea behind juror deliberations is you’re supposed to discuss the evidence with one another with candor, to openly listen. But it sounds like there was one juror whose position is intractable, that they have this fixed view that isn’t changing, no matter how much more deliberations continue. When it comes to reasonable doubt, if a juror actually believes there’s reasonable doubt, that’s supposed to work in Clancy’s favor. That’s the whole point of the standard. Reasonable doubt isn’t something you just note and move past. It’s supposed to end the jury’s deliberations in the defendant’s favor. Reddington suggested that the holdout juror did acknowledge doubt but still wanted to convict anyway. That’s his whole argument, that this one juror wasn’t following the law as instructed and he wanted to have him removed. But that being said, you have to keep in mind that we are getting this information secondhand—it’s a juror note that’s being interpreted and characterized by Reddington. This is not a note from the holdout juror. It’s coming from the point of view of jurors who are frustrated with this lone juror who is not agreeing with them and their characterization of this juror. How unusual is it for there to be one holdout juror that ends up overtaking a jury’s decisionmaking like this? I think it’s less unusual than people think. Hung juries do happen across criminal trials; obviously, they are more rare and infrequent. However, when a jury hangs, it’s usually in this exact sort of situation, where it’s not an even split—it’s one person who won’t move. It is also common and happens throughout every jury trial that jurors send notes. That’s how they communicate with the judge and the lawyers. Some of those notes might ask that they see certain evidence again, they might want to see certain transcripts, they might need the judge to instruct them on the law. Sometimes, through questions that jurors are asking, both the judge and the lawyers might gain some insights into what the jurors are thinking in a particular case, and it certainly seems that that’s what happened here. My understanding is that Reddington has, through these notes, come to his own assessment that except for this holdout juror, there’s an 11-to-1 split leaning toward not guilty by reason of lack of criminal responsibility. We know that Reddington asked the judge to have this one juror thrown out, but the judge said no. What considerations do judges make in these types of situations? It’s certainly rare that somebody’s trying to remove one juror at this stage of the case, and it’s important to grasp why he’s trying to do that. The only way to do it is by saying: Look, this juror is not following his oath. He’s not able to follow the law. That’s my understanding that was gleaned from one of the juror notes. However, simply because the juror is holding out and not agreeing with other jurors, that’s not a basis to remove them. It’s a very delicate balance here because this whole system works by respecting the integrity of the deliberations and the process. If this one juror just had strong convictions that happen to be different from everyone else’s, the law allows that, and it’s not for a judge or anyone else to intervene after the jury has been instructed to try to come to a consensus. The judge’s only role here is to make sure the jurors are following the law, and unless he had reason to believe that this juror was not following and applying the law, he was not going to intervene. If it was so clear-cut that the juror was actually saying, “I have reasonable doubt, but I want to convict,” the judge’s decision about whether there was good cause to remove the juror would have been much more straightforward. But that’s clearly not what happened here. I don’t think it was as simple as Reddington, perhaps, is characterizing the note, or as the media is characterizing the note. Remember, Reddington seemed to think that but for this lone juror, the verdict would have been in his client’s favor. He went down the only avenue that was available to him after he made his motion to appeal to the judge, and that motion was denied. My understanding is that it had a very low chance of success, but as a defense attorney you have to do everything you can for your client and to exhaust every possible avenue, and he did that. Reddington’s appeal failed, and Sullivan officially declared a mistrial. What might the prosecution do next? There are three options. They can retry the case with a brand-new jury. Or they could negotiate some sort of plea deal with Clancy. My understanding is that there might be limitations on what pleas are available based on the counts that are in this case. The last option would be to not pursue this case further. My understanding is that the district attorney’s office hasn’t said anything publicly yet about how they intend to pursue. My sense is, given how much public attention this case has gotten, they are going to retry her. It’s hard to envision that the prosecutors learned anything different about this trial. Presumably these were the choices that they had before, they could have tried her or offered a plea deal, and I don’t think the trial produced any new information that the prosecution learned that changed their view of this case. They believe that this was intentional and premeditated, and I think they’re going to stand by those beliefs. I don’t think the public attention is going to sway the prosecution, but finding a new jury who is impartial is going to be really challenging, because this case has received so much media attention. I think in the future, attorneys would try to screen this out during jury selection. You can ask questions to try to screen this out, and it’s worth pointing out that this is not the first time—there are cases that have received so much media attention. Those cases do get tried, but it’s certainly challenging. The DA presumably makes the final decision to retry Clancy. He’ll likely consult with others in his office, but ultimately it’s his decision to make. And he would try the same exact charges but have a new jury. She has been denied bail, so she’s been held in custody since 2023 at the Tewksbury State Hospital. Now that a mistrial has been declared, that does not equate to an acquittal. Nothing changes, at least immediately, in terms of where she stands; she’ll continue staying at Tewksbury. Her lawyer might try to have her bail reconsidered, but the fact that there’s been a mistrial doesn’t really change Clancy’s circumstances. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. US watchdog opens probe into Tesla's Cybercab self-certification
2026-09-07

US watchdog opens probe into Tesla's Cybercab self-certification

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Wheels turning on the road and in government Tesla's Cybercab's wheels have only just hit the asphalt for its commercial launch and the autonomous vehicle has already attracted the attention of the US National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA). The body announced an investigation into Tesla's self-certification of the vehicle at the weekend. Central to the probe is the fact that the Cybercab famously lacks conventional controls. The NHTSA says there are "clear vehicle performance requirements" – stuff like brake controls – that "in order for automakers to access the US market, they must certify that their vehicles meet these standards – subject to oversight from the agency to confirm compliance. When certified vehicles appear to not adhere to these requirements, NHTSA conducts an investigation." The NHTSA is updating the requirements for autonomous vehicles designed never to be operated by a human, but Tesla appears to have jumped the gun somewhat, prompting the agency to open an Audit Query (AQ) just hours after the Cybercab began a commercial rollout on the streets of Austin, Texas. Tesla told the NHTSA that it had certified the Cybercab "as compliant with all applicable Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards (FMVSS)." The vehicles lack permanently attached manual controls such as a brake pedal, accelerator, steering wheel, or mirrors. In the AQ, the agency wrote it intended to "examine the process and technical data on which Tesla relied when certifying the Cybercab and related issues. "Among other things, NHTSA will consider the extent to which Tesla’s certification depended on determinations that certain FMVSS are inapplicable to the Cybercab." Tesla is not the only company with autonomous vehicles ferrying passengers around on US roads without using conventional controls in the cabin. Amazon tentacle Zoox, for example, was granted a two-year exemption [PDF] at the end of July 2026, with the proviso that "not more than 2,500 exempted vehicles are introduced into interstate commerce for commercial deployment in any 12-month period." There is therefore precedent. However, the NHTSA wants to see how Tesla came up with its self-certification for the Cybercab. NHTSA Administrator Jonathan Morrison said, "NHTSA fully supports the safe development and deployment of automated vehicles. But as the federal regulator, we need to ensure that all of our laws are followed." The agency noted, "When certified vehicles appear to not adhere to these requirements, NHTSA conducts an investigation." ® Wheels turning on the road and in government PC buyers already had enough of helping to fund the AI revolution, only essential refreshes happening now Nothing says ‘For internal use only’ quite like emailing it to the press 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Commission reportedly seeks third-party views, although no formal investigation is underway AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. DRAM contract prices forecast to grow only 13-18% in Q3
2026-09-07

DRAM contract prices forecast to grow only 13-18% in Q3

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
PC buyers already had enough of helping to fund the AI revolution, only essential refreshes happening now The relentless rise in memory prices may finally be losing steam, as PC and smartphone buyers reach the limits of what their budgets will allow them to pay. Taiwanese market watcher Trend Force estimates that rising DRAM contract prices drove a 59.5 percent quarter-on-quarter jump in industry revenue to $154.73 billion in calendar Q2. The analyst now expects conventional DRAM contract price growth to "moderate" to 13 to 18 percent quarter-on-quarter in Q3. That reflects a shift in demand away from high-capacity RDIMMs toward lower-capacity products, as well as "the limited ability of PC and smartphone customers to absorb further price increases." That 13 to 18 percent is still a punishing increase by any normal standard. Supplier inventories remain at historic lows, TrendForce warns, and total bit shipments are expected to grow modestly. In other words, prices are climbing more slowly and supplies are likely to remain tight for the forseeable future. The forecast follows a warning from market intelligence biz Context that European PC shipments are set to decline even more sharply as rising component costs push up system prices. Context forecasts a year-on-year decline in laptop shipments of 6.4 percent in Q3, followed by a 20 percent fall in Q4. The outlook is even worse for desktops, with shipments expected to decline around 20 percent in Q3 and almost 30 percent in Q4. Despite declining shipments, PC makers have continued to bring home the bacon, as higher prices have so far more than compensated for the fall in volumes. Earlier this year, The Register reported how Lenovo boasted it had sidestepped the fallout from the memory crisis by shifting its focus to premium devices. Context says corporate buyers are extending refresh cycles where possible and acquiring new PCs only when upgrades are unavoidable. "The PC refresh cycle has not disappeared, but the economics around it have changed," said Context senior analyst Marie-Christine Pygott. "For the channel, the opportunity is increasingly about helping customers prioritize where refreshes are essential, where devices can remain in service for longer and where investment in newer, more capable systems makes commercial sense." As for the AI PCs that manufacturers hoped would trigger a mass upgrade wave, Context says the end of Windows 10 support was the principal driver of recent buying activity, as The Register noted at the start of this year. While AI-capable PCs account for an increasing share of systems entering the European supply chain, this is mostly because AI functionality is becoming standard in newer devices. TrendForce notes that the three major memory makers continue to focus on high-end products for the AI market. Samsung benefited from its early move into HBM4 mass production, while SK hynix had the highest proportion of HBM in its total bit shipments among the three leading suppliers. Micron is prioritizing higher-priced server DRAM. That has left second-tier manufacturers such as Nanya, Winbond, and PSMC to meet demand for mature-process products such as DDR4 and DDR3. ® Wheels turning on the road and in government PC buyers already had enough of helping to fund the AI revolution, only essential refreshes happening now Nothing says ‘For internal use only’ quite like emailing it to the press 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Commission reportedly seeks third-party views, although no formal investigation is underway AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. Oracle may be next in EU software licensing hot seat following SAP deal
2026-09-07

Oracle may be next in EU software licensing hot seat following SAP deal

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Commission reportedly seeks third-party views, although no formal investigation is underway The European Commission (EC) may be laying the groundwork for an investigation into Oracle's software licensing practices, according to reports. The scrutiny follows the EC's investigation into fellow enterprise software vendor SAP, which ended with the Commission accepting legally binding commitments from the German company to abolish reinstatement fees and reduce back-maintenance fees, among other concessions. MLex is now reporting that the EC – the EU's executive arm – has started to examine Oracle's licensing practices ahead of a possible formal investigation. One source told the publication that the EC was seeking views from third parties. An EC spokesperson told The Register it had concluded an investigation into SAP, and "will continue to monitor possible further anti-competitive practices and abusive conduct in this sector. At this stage however, there is no formal investigation into any company." Big Red's licensing practices have attracted criticism for years, although the vendor maintains they are fair. One frequently criticized example is its treatment of Java, which came under Oracle's stewardship after it agreed to buy Sun Microsystems in 2009. OpenJDK, the reference implementation of the Java platform, is open source, but vendors can charge for proprietary development kits, runtime environments, and support. Oracle announced a new licensing regime for Oracle Java SE in January 2023. It said Java SE Universal Subscription would offer customers "a simple, low-cost monthly subscription that includes Java SE Licensing and Support for use on Desktops, Servers or Cloud deployments." Industry experts pointed out that even businesses with limited use of Oracle Java could have to pay according to their total employee count under the model, a dramatic departure from Oracle's previous licensing terms. Gartner estimated the per-employee subscription model to be two to five times more expensive than the legacy version. Another licensing mechanism that has attracted criticism is the Unlimited License Agreement. Some licensing advisors claim the all-you-can-eat option offers poor value, locks users in, and limits their choice of alternative products. Oracle maintains that the agreement offers flexibility and good value. The EC accepted SAP's commitments in July after opening an investigation into concerns that the German vendor may have restricted competition in the aftermarket for maintenance and support of its on-premises ERP software. Gartner welcomed the commitments, saying they gave customers more options for their legacy SAP applications by making third-party support a more viable alternative to migration. Vendor support for some older SAP products is due to end in 2027. ® Wheels turning on the road and in government PC buyers already had enough of helping to fund the AI revolution, only essential refreshes happening now Nothing says ‘For internal use only’ quite like emailing it to the press 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own Commission reportedly seeks third-party views, although no formal investigation is underway AI workstation promises to put up to 576 GB of HBM3e and 16 TB/s of memory bandwidth on your desk $12.9 billion deal will inevitably cement Nvidia's market dominance and harm competition in the process. The Verge
Six years later, Sony revisits its legendary XM4 headphones
2026-09-07

Six years later, Sony revisits its legendary XM4 headphones

The Verge | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
After the WH-1000XM4 were discontinued in 2025, Sony is bringing them back as the WH-1000XM4C. After the WH-1000XM4 were discontinued in 2025, Sony is bringing them back as the WH-1000XM4C. Six years ago Sony and Bose were in the middle of a noise-canceling battle, with each new model of headphones better than the last. In the fall of 2020, Sony released the WH-1000XM4 headphones to wide acclaim. They sat atop best headphones lists for years, thanks to their great sound, competitive ANC, and compact size, until they went out of stock. Now Sony has brought them back, with minor improvements, as the WH-1000XM4C. The XM4C look identical to the original XM4 (although they now have a lavender option), with the same hinge design and ability to fold up. It’s a compact design that Sony moved away from when it released the XM5 in 2022, and then returned to (although not quite as compact) with the XM6. But it’s not just the look that’s returned. The XM4C still use the same QN1 processing chip and four microphones for ANC. That design is showing its age: The XM5 still used the QN1, but had eight mics. Last year’s XM6 have 12, plus the new QN3 chip. Sony did update the XM4C’s sound profile to bring it in line with current models, and the headphones have a 10-band EQ in the Headphones app, instead of the 5-band on the XM4, plus a gaming preset. The XM4C also support Bluetooth 6 instead of 5. Surprisingly, the battery performance on the new headphones is slightly worse. Sony claims the XM4C get up to 27 hours of use with ANC on as opposed to 30 hours previously. Not a huge difference, and still more than enough for a cross-country trip. The XM4C will be available later this fall for $300, which is $50 cheaper than the XM4 when they first came out (and $100 cheaper than the XM6 are now), but more than they could be found for before being discontinued. I have loved the Sony XM4 since they came out, and still have my pair hanging on the side of my desk. Beyond nostalgia, though, I’m confused why they’re coming back. A review sample of the XM4C arrived at my door while I was writing this, so some deeper investigation will be coming soon. But the headphones industry has moved forward in the past six years, and I’m not sure there’s a need to go back. And the XM5, while they don’t fold up as nicely as the XM4C, are excellent headphones and currently available for $200 (though that price may be an effort to clear out inventory ahead of the XM4C). Alongside the XM4C, Sony released the next generation of two of its less-expensive midrange and entry-level headphone lines: the WH-CH730N and WH-CH530. They both get the same 10-band EQ upgrade and gaming EQ preset as the XM4C, and the CH730N will now fold up into a more compact form. The two headphones also get a boost to battery life according to Sony: The CH730N get up to 50 hours with ANC, and the CH530 get up to 55 hours (there’s no ANC on the CH530). A new safe mode in the app allows a user to limit the max volume output of the headphones. It’s a nice addition for parents looking to protect their kids’ hearing, although there’s no way to lock the limit once it’s been set. The WH-CH530 come in black, cobalt, mint green, white, pink, and coral and cost $70. For $180, you can get the WH-CN730N in black, blue, oat, white, or pink. Both headphones are available now. Sony is also doing a partnership with Hello Kitty. Bundles include headphones — the WH-1000XM6, WH-CH730N, or WH-CH530 available in black or pink — a Hello Kitty-shaped carrying pouch, and Hello Kitty-branded bag. The bundles will be available in limited supply later this year on the Sony website only. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. EcoFlow makes the miniature power station even smaller

EcoFlow makes the miniature power station even smaller

The Verge | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
More energy packed into far less space makes these batteries ideal for day trips or weekend getaways. More energy packed into far less space makes these batteries ideal for day trips or weekend getaways. If you’re in the market for a tiny power station that punches well above its size and weight then have a look at EcoFlow’s new fourth-generation River series. The River 260 Gen4 features a 256Wh capacity battery while the 520 Gen4 packs in 512Wh — storing about 2.5x and 5x the energy of the largest airline-approved power banks, respectively. Better yet, the batteries are built using longer-lasting LFP chemistry that’s far less volatile. The River 260 Gen4 features four USB ports, and 300W of max continuous output delivered out its lone AC jack. The River 500 Gen4 features the same USB port selection but offers a pair of AC jacks good for 500W of continuous output. Both of EcoFlow’s Gen4 power stations feature class-leading energy density that cuts both the weight and size, making these power stations lighter and more compact than similarly-specced competition. That makes them ideal for an overnight camping trip or photo shoot in the literal field. The EcoFlow 520 Gen4 is powerful enough to keep a standard US fridge running for about 4 hours during a blackout. Here’s a quick comparison to show just how much energy these remarkably small power stations are packing, starting with the River 520 Gen4: And here’s how the River 260 Gen4 compares to some of the main competition: Both models can serve as a UPS for your computer, with a cutover time of less than 10ms, and feature one state-of-the-art 140W USB-C PD3.1 port that’s bidirectional. That little trick lets these power stations eschew charging bricks in favor of direct DC charging from a 140W USB-C wall charger you might already own, or a portable USB-C solar panel EcoFlow will happily sell you. EcoFlow has also addressed a common problem with all power stations that causes them to eventually die if left idling too long. By minimizing the energy spent converting stored DC to AC output, more watt-hours remain to charge your drone, bike computer, and phone, or to power your Starlink Mini and compact fridge. The River 260 Gen4 will be available in Europe from Q4 2026, while the River 520 Gen4 will follow sometimes in Q1 2027. Pricing for both will be announced as we get closer to their global release dates. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. The Verge
The real reason your phone is getting more expensive
2026-09-07

The real reason your phone is getting more expensive

The Verge | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
Soaring RAM costs are driving up prices across the tech industry. It may take years to settle down. Soaring RAM costs are driving up prices across the tech industry. It may take years to settle down. When Apple debuts the next generation of iPhones this week, they’re likely to come with an unwanted change: a higher price tag. A price hike from the supply-chain powerhouse would be the clearest sign yet that soaring memory costs have become unavoidable — with no end to the memory crunch in sight. Call it “chipflation” or “RAMageddon.” The shortage is reversing a decades-long decline in memory costs that helped make consumer electronics more powerful without making them dramatically more expensive. The terms “memory prices” and “memory shortage” appeared in 473 company transcripts last quarter, according to data provided by AlphaSense. The entire industry is seemingly working on the problem. And yet, we’re years away from seeing it fixed. The common wisdom is that AI is at fault: The technology isn’t just contributing to higher energy prices and layoffs; it’s also helping drive up the cost of everything from smartphones to game consoles. But the shortage was brewing well before ChatGPT took off — and was then amplified by AI’s unending hunger for RAM. The current market is the product of a complicated and very profitable reshuffling of the memory industry within a system that was already failing to keep up. “We need to build more wafer capacity,” Manish Bhatia, president and COO of Micron — one of the three largest memory manufacturers — told The Verge. “[It’s] a very different challenge for the industry than it had been for many years before, where technology alone was able to keep up with the demand.” For years, memory manufacturers could increase production by fitting more chips onto each wafer. But those gains were shrinking and taking longer to achieve. In 2021, even as pandemic-era electronics demand surged, Micron concluded that the problem was more fundamental: Technological advances alone would no longer create enough capacity to keep pace with long-term demand. Manufacturers would have to process more wafers — and build enormous new facilities to do it. Then the memory business collapsed. Pandemic-era purchases of computers, tablets, and phones had pulled demand forward; consumer spending weakened, and manufacturers were left with excess inventory. They lost money and slowed their expansion plans. By the time the market began recovering, generative AI had unleashed a wave of demand far larger and more memory-intensive than manufacturers had ever anticipated. The memory business is also extraordinarily concentrated. Three manufacturers account for about 90 percent of the market, according to Counterpoint, leaving the world dependent on a handful of companies to divide limited capacity between AI infrastructure and consumer devices. Counterpoint estimates that Samsung controlled 39 percent of the memory market in the second quarter of 2026, followed by SK Hynix at 26 percent and Micron at 25 percent. The memory inside your phone or computer broadly falls into two buckets. DRAM, or dynamic random-access memory, temporarily holds the information a device needs while opening apps, loading webpages, or running software. NAND flash memory provides longer-term storage for things like photos and files. “It’s not as simple as saying data centers are consuming RAM. The RAM is not the same.” Inside AI data centers, specialized processors rely heavily on a form of DRAM called high-bandwidth memory, or HBM. “It’s not as simple as saying data centers are consuming RAM,” David Naranjo, associate director at Counterpoint, told The Verge. “The RAM is not the same.” By stacking memory chips together and using advanced connections and packaging, HBM can move enormous quantities of data to and from those processors much faster and more efficiently. It’s more difficult to produce, but also more lucrative to sell. Deep-pocketed AI chipmakers like Nvidia and AMD and tech giants like Meta and Microsoft have an insatiable need for it to power their AI systems — and are more than happy to pay. HBM also consumes significantly more manufacturing capacity than conventional DRAM. Memory chips are made many at a time on large, circular silicon wafers. Because a finished HBM product stacks multiple, larger chips together, it requires considerably more silicon. Micron estimates that producing a given amount of HBM requires roughly three times as many wafers as producing the same amount of conventional DRAM. The incentives increasingly favor AI. Rather than guessing how many phones or laptops will sell a year from now, memory makers can lock in multiyear commitments from some of the world’s richest companies. “We have been engaging in discussions with customers, prioritizing those who can guarantee committed future captive demand,” Jaejune Kim, Samsung’s executive vice president of memory, said on the company’s last earnings call. AI is also increasing demand for conventional DRAM. Phone and PC makers want to run smaller AI models directly on their devices, requiring more sophisticated conventional memory — and more of it. Future models may need substantially more memory than their predecessors to support those capabilities. “The three big memory guys, they’re just basically allocating the capacity that they have to these companies.” “As AI spreads across various services such as search, coding, and productivity tools, the scope of demand is widening from a memory perspective,” said Song Hyun-jong, president of SK Hynix, another of the big three memory manufacturers, on the company’s most recent earnings call. “We are witnessing a structural shift in demand where both AI memory and conventional memory are growing together.” Samsung and Micron have both said they remain committed to conventional DRAM. Micron’s Bhatia told The Verge that it still accounts for most of the company’s wafer capacity. But limited supply, surging demand, and AI customers willing to make long-term commitments have made it extraordinarily lucrative to cede space to HBM. SK Hynix posted a record 76 percent operating margin last quarter, up from 41 percent a year earlier, while Micron’s adjusted gross margin hit a record 85 percent. Samsung’s semiconductor profits, meanwhile, jumped roughly 250-fold from a year earlier. “It’s more profitable,” Naranjo said. “The three big memory guys, they’re just basically allocating the capacity that they have to these companies.” Counterpoint estimates that DRAM prices for smartphones increased roughly 56 percent in the first quarter of 2026 compared to a quarter earlier, and grew around 83 percent in the second quarter. For 16GB of DRAM for a smartphone, Counterpoint estimates that it cost roughly $42 in the second quarter of 2025 and about $181 a year later — an increase of more than 300 percent. Those aren’t necessarily Apple’s prices — few companies have more leverage with suppliers — but the figures show how dramatically the component costs of building a high-end phone have changed. The obvious solution is to make more memory. The problem is that it takes years to accomplish — even if you’re working fast. Micron is currently demonstrating just how much work “more” entails. In July, the company poured the first concrete for its planned manufacturing complex near Syracuse in upstate New York. Once completed, the site will contain 2.4 million square feet of cleanroom space, making it the largest semiconductor manufacturing site in US history by that measure — and Micron’s largest facility anywhere in the world. Those cleanrooms where the chips are made will occupy only a fraction of the entire build, which is roughly the size of 350 football fields. “By the time we’re done, we’ll have built probably 15 to 20 million actual square feet of building space to support the 2.4 million square feet of cleanroom,” Bhatia told The Verge. In one of Micron’s fab designs, the cleanroom occupies just one floor of a five-story building. Micron doesn’t expect meaningful output until 2030 The remaining space will house a vast network of water-treatment, power, air-handling, and utility-distribution systems needed to keep it running. Even though Micron reached the concrete pour ahead of schedule, it still took more than two years. The company began permitting after its CHIPS Act grant for New York was confirmed in March 2024, broke ground in January 2026, and poured concrete about six months later. The physical construction unfolds in three broad stages. Preparing the ground can take six to nine months, Bhatia said. Building the concrete structure and facade can require another 18 months. But the most complicated stage comes after the building is standing: installing the vast network of mechanical, electrical, plumbing, and piping systems required to operate a semiconductor fab. Those systems must turn an enormous industrial building into an environment where a single microscopic particle doesn’t land in the wrong place and ruin a chip. Bhatia offered a sense of the precision required: “Imagine trying to build all of New York state and not have any ants anywhere in the entire state,” he said. “That’s what we’re trying to do on every one of these wafers, and we do it over and over again.” Completing the building still doesn’t produce memory. Micron expects to activate and test essential systems like power, water, and ventilation at the end of 2028, then spend 2029 moving in manufacturing equipment and beginning pilot production. The company doesn’t expect meaningful output until 2030. Even on an accelerated timeline, the journey from permitting to production will have taken roughly six years. The company’s new Idaho fab is further along and is expected to begin wafer output in mid-2027. Micron is spending enormous amounts to move the process along more quickly, roughly doubling its capital expenditure from a year earlier to exceed $25 billion this year. Even with that investment, Micron can’t identify a clear point at which supply will catch up because demand continues to grow almost as quickly as manufacturers can expand. “We’ve said that right now it’ll be beyond 2027, and we don’t see when it closes just because demand continues to grow so fast,” Bhatia said. Counterpoint doesn’t expect capacity to catch up with demand until late 2027 or early 2028 in the best-case scenario. The market data firm IDC similarly expects the shortage to last through 2027 and well into early 2028 before there is meaningful relief. PC and smartphone makers can’t absorb component costs that have multiplied several times over indefinitely. They can raise prices. They can make fewer devices. They can put less memory in them. Or they can focus on more expensive devices with enough profit margin to absorb the added cost. Citing soaring memory costs, Microsoft recently raised Xbox prices by another $100 to $150, leaving some models as much as $300 more expensive than at launch. Microsoft also raised the price of some Surface Pro laptops and tablets by $500 over their original starting price. Meta, meanwhile, added $100 to the cost of its Quest 3 headset. Apple is perhaps the most revealing test case because it should be among the companies best equipped to withstand the shortage. Its scale gives it extraordinary leverage with suppliers, and its customers have historically proved willing to pay premium prices. Yet Apple has already raised prices across its lineup of Macs and iPads. Rather than releasing its full lineup at once this fall, Apple is expected to introduce the iPhone 18 Pro and Pro Max alongside its first foldable iPhone, while holding the standard iPhone 18 and refreshed Air until spring 2027, according to Bloomberg. Tim Cook described memory pricing as a “100-year flood” That leaves Apple’s fall lineup concentrated heavily at the most expensive end of the market — exactly where it has the most room to protect its margins. Counterpoint’s supply-chain tracking shows Apple preparing roughly 10 percent more iPhone 18 Pro and Pro Max units for September through December than it allocated to the iPhone 17 Pro and Pro Max during the same period last year. On Apple’s last earnings call, the company forecast slower growth due partly to worsening supply constraints, including for memory chips, and said higher memory costs had directly reduced its gross margin. Then-CEO Tim Cook described memory pricing as a “100-year flood,” citing “exponential increases” that had caused Apple to raise prices. The iPhone 18 Pro could start at $1,299, according to The Wall Street Journal’s calculation based on estimates from TechInsights that factor in component costs and attempt to minimize margin reductions. That would be $200 more than the iPhone 17 Pro. Samsung and Google have each raised the starting prices of some new phones by $100 this year, though several also come with more storage. Apple may be better positioned than most companies to pull off higher prices. Its installed base, tightly integrated ecosystem, and loyal customers make its phones behave more like luxury goods than interchangeable electronics. The company has already gained market share by keeping its prices steady while lower-end competitors raised theirs. In places like China, the world’s largest smartphone market, it almost became common sense to opt for a premium iPhone when lower-cost alternatives were selling for nearly as much. For other manufacturers, the options are worse. Counterpoint and IDC are already seeing smartphone companies reduce expected shipments while shifting toward more expensive models. If they can’t sell as many phones, they can at least try to extract more revenue from each one. That means more OLED displays, AI features, larger memory configurations, and other upgrades that can justify higher prices. As a result, revenue for PCs and smartphones could actually remain flat or even rise as unit sales decline. In other words, AI isn’t just making electronics cost more. The memory shortage is changing which electronics companies bother making in the first place. Samsung, SK Hynix, and Micron are all investing heavily in additional manufacturing. But because new facilities take years to build, that spending won’t bring immediate relief. SK Hynix is investing 600 trillion won in its Yongin Semiconductor Cluster, a massive complex south of Seoul first announced in 2019, and has accelerated its target for completion from 2045 to 2033. The company says it will double its capacity within five years but still expects demand to outpace supply through 2030. Samsung and SK Hynix also plan to invest a combined 800 trillion won, or about $588 billion, in four new memory-chip factories in southwestern South Korea, according to The Wall Street Journal. Micron’s New York and Idaho projects are part of the more than $250 billion it says it will spend on US manufacturing and research through 2035. A potentially important fourth player is also emerging in China. CXMT is building additional fabs, though its technology isn’t yet considered as advanced as that of Samsung, SK Hynix, or Micron. Apple is currently testing CXMT chips but would need approval from the Trump administration to do business with the company. The other possibility is that supply won’t have to catch up because demand will collapse first. If the AI investment bubble bursts, memory manufacturers could suddenly find themselves facing very different economics. For now, that remains hypothetical. The long-term contracts AI companies have signed with memory suppliers make the immediate future easier to predict. “2027 is pretty locked and loaded,” Naranjo said. Counterpoint and IDC expect price increases to slow from the breathtaking pace of the past year to single or low double digits. But slower increases aren’t the same as falling prices. “Not to say prices will come down to 2025 levels, but the price increases will stabilize at a new normal,” Nabila Popal, a senior director at IDC, told The Verge. That new normal is “at least triple what they used to be.” New fabs still take years to come online, while AI data centers and increasingly sophisticated consumer devices continue to require more memory. And manufacturers have little economic incentive to prioritize cheaper consumer electronics over customers willing to pay handsomely and commit years in advance. The Verge
Bentley’s Torcal EV tries to balance authenticity with fake V8 sounds
2026-09-07

Bentley’s Torcal EV tries to balance authenticity with fake V8 sounds

The Verge | 2026-09-07

Visit Original Website
The British automaker is on the hunt for a new demographic with its first electric vehicle. The British automaker is on the hunt for a new demographic with its first electric vehicle. Thanks to their ability to provide a smooth ride and quiet powertrain with ease, electric vehicles are a true shoo-in for the high-end luxury automotive segment. Rolls-Royce has the Spectre, Cadillac’s Celestiq is taking bespoke to a whole new level, and Mercedes has given the EQS the full Maybach treatment. From Volkswagen Auto Group, Bentley is just about to debut its 2027 Torcal, a spacious SUV that shares a platform with the electrified Porsche Cayenne. I recently got a quick ride in a heavily camouflaged preproduction Torcal as a way to experience it before its official debut toward the end of this month. Between this brute’s spacious interior, supple ride quality, and focus on craftsmanship in the digital age, all signs point to it not just being a viable competitor in the segment, but also characteristically Bentley. When I say heavily camouflaged, this not only covers (pun intended) the Torcal’s exterior, but the interior as well. Thick black cloth was draped over most of it, to the point of the engineers punching holes in the cloth for proper seatbelt usage. The exterior was rather deceiving, too; apparently they affixed false body paneling under the wrap so as not to fully spoil its overall shape and profile. Upon first glance, it seemed like Bentley took a page from Lucid’s Gravity by making it wider at the front than at the back—in the name of teardrop-resembling aerodynamics—but Martin Page, the Torcal’s product line director-slash-chauffeur for my ride, assured me that what I was seeing (or rather, trying to see) wouldn’t be production. However, this enabled me to zero in closer on what I could feel and hear. Whether up front or in back, the Torcal is amply spacious, even though it’s noticeably smaller in size and shape than Bentley’s current Bentayga. Page explained the great lengths that engineers reached to ensure it had an isolating and tremendously smooth ride. Running off-the-shelf two-chamber air suspension — common in the rest of the brand’s lineup — would seemingly suffice with an EV’s inherently good chassis balance, but engineers took it a step further by carefully altering the flow of oil through its adaptive dampers. This means that the jump in ride and handling between Comfort and Sport modes is much more discernible. No matter the mode, it rode incredibly smoothly. Heavy focus was paid to the Torcal’s alignment, too, with steering that returns to and maintains center as confidently as possible. Diving deeper into signature Bentley characteristics, developing the best simulated engine note was high on the list of priorities. Inspiration came from the brand’s V8-powered Turbo R of the 1980s, except the track itself was recorded by a musician’s drum beat and mixed accordingly, rather than simply churned out from a synthesizer. “What we wanted to do throughout the car is maintain craftsmanship, not only in the materials but also in the way we execute the digital aspects,” Page shared. “What we wanted to do throughout the car is maintain craftsmanship, not only in the materials but also in the way we execute the digital aspects.” The reasoning behind this lies in hearing subtle imperfections, much like one would from an analogue internal combustion powertrain. Going one step further, the Torcal’s throttle tuning seemed to mimic a torquey V8 with progressive power delivery. In light of this, Page added that with this sound turned on, it gives occupants auditory cues to tie to the vehicle’s torque delivery, which is known to help reduce motion sickness. The sound has not yet been finalized and is still being finely tweaked at the time of this writing. I got a peek at the digital instrument cluster and center infotainment screen — Page had to observe the speed limit, after all. The image these display in the background is of a fine crystal decanter hand-cut by Cumbria Crystal with light shone through it. In Page’s words, this shows the natural refractions and reflections that you get from every one of the cuts. It’s another indication of the brand’s focus on craftsmanship in the digital age, albeit a subtle one. It’s also worth pointing out that Bentley takes net environmental impact seriously. “In this car we take sustainability to another level, we have clear targets for circular material and green aluminum,” Page shared. “The door cards are made with a natural fiber — not injection-molded plastic — that’s fully recyclable and fully sustainable.” Then, Bentley is working with fellow UK company Fox Brothers to offer a Merino wool interior option, not just as a lesser-taken route for comfort (Volvo’s done well with wool, too) but also as a sturdy, renewable material that will hold up to automotive use. Finally, the assembly plant in Crewe, England, has tough requirements for green energy use (namely solar) and other renewable practices. Output, battery, and charging specs are not carved into stone just yet. Power will be north of 800 hp and 700 lb ft of torque, and curb weight is estimated to be nearly 6,000 lbs. Page was quiet about battery size (though, expect it to mirror the Porsche’s 108 net kWh unit), and the targeted range is over 370 miles. With a peak charging rate of 400 kW, the brand is aiming for a 10–80 percent top-up in under 20 minutes, with 100 miles tacking on in as little as seven. “We’re trying to be authentic in the whole experience,” Page summed up as we headed back to the parking lot where I met him. The approach with the Torcal is to make a great Bentley first, and in the process of doing so, a great EV. It combines some already hashed-out tech from Volkswagen, but the brand is hoping its focus on craftsmanship and fine-tuning will attract discerning, well-heeled potential customers who want an electric luxury vehicle. Pricing hasn’t been announced yet, but it will be interesting to see how the Torcal is received after its full official unveiling. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. ﻿The Mate XT 2 also borrows Samsung’s inward-folding design. ﻿The Mate XT 2 also borrows Samsung’s inward-folding design. Huawei has released its third trifold phone in China, and the company has clearly had half an eye on Samsung during development. Not only does the Mate XT 2 adopt the inward-folding form factor used on Samsung’s Galaxy Z TriFold, it’s also the first foldable to include a privacy-enhancing display, much like the one Samsung added to the Galaxy S26 Ultra this year. The first Mate XT, which launched in China in late 2024 and internationally a few months later, used an accordion-esque folding design. It removed the need to include an additional cover screen on the phone, but left parts of the delicate folding display permanently exposed. The follow-up XTs retained the design, but the XT 2 instead takes inspiration from Samsung’s trifold, folding both sides of the 10.2-inch flexible screen inwards to keep them safe from harm. More surprising is the addition of a privacy OLED display. This looks much like Samsung’s, so far only found on the S26 Ultra. When activated, it switches on pixels that emit light at narrower angles, making the screen appear almost black from the sides, but still visible head-on. It’s an optional extra, only available if you opt for 16GB of RAM and 1TB of storage and pay a further ¥1,000 (about $150) to add the privacy screen. Beyond the display, the XT 2 has Huawei’s new Kirin 9050 Pro chipset, improved water-resistance with an IP59 rating, and a triple rear camera. It launched today in China, starting at ¥19,999 ($3,000), though international release plans remain unknown. Samsung’s own Galaxy Z TriFold was discontinued shortly after its release last year, though leaks suggest that Samsung is still working on a follow-up.
